_source line 5752 · 2026-05-24T18:30:22.487154+00:00_

8:We stratify SWE-bench Verified instances by F2P test count $T$, comparing per-test vs.\ trajectory-level oracle resolve rates under i.i.d.\ and block-correlated error models (ICC $= 0.191$, from function-level analysis; \S\ref{sec:equivalence}).
13:\caption{Oracle accuracy-utility frontier: per-test advantage stratified by F2P test count ($T$) on SWE-bench Verified (500 instances). Block-correlated model uses function-level ICC $= 0.191$ (from function-level analysis, \S\ref{sec:equivalence}). P2P regression rate: 1.04\% mean, 0\% median.}
34:\caption{Oracle per-test advantage by F2P test count $T$. Advantage is zero at $T{=}1$ (68.7\%) and concentrates at $T{=}2$ (19.1\%, contributing 55.3\% of total advantage). Note: $y$-axis does not start at zero.}
38:[Omitted long matching line]
379:Multi-F2P instances (6--10 tests) reach 96.3\% accuracy, consistent with the oracle finding that per-test granularity is most valuable for complex instances.

451:Table~\ref{tab:variant_power} reports variant-level bootstrap confidence intervals for the repo-level formulation comparisons ($n{=}48$ variants). Following Hoenig and Heisey~\cite{hoenig2001}, we report minimum detectable effect (MDE) rather than post-hoc observed power.
456:\caption{Variant-level bootstrap analysis ($n{=}48$). \cls{}--\cwm{} uses paired bootstrap (same variants, same seed); \cls{}--\traj{} uses unpaired bootstrap (per-variant \traj{} predictions not archived for pairing). MDE at $n{=}48$: ${\sim}$25\pp{}.}
476:At $n{=}48$, the MDE is ${\sim}$25\pp{}. The \cls{}--\cwm{} gap (29--35\pp{}) exceeds this threshold, confirming that $n{=}48$ is sufficient to detect the observed divergence. The \cls{}--\traj{} gaps ($<$10\pp{}) fall well below the MDE: any true difference is too small to detect at this sample size, consistent with the function-level TOST equivalence (\S\ref{sec:equivalence}).

440	
441	\subsection{Bayesian ROPE Analysis}
442	\label{app:bayesian_rope}
443	To complement the frequentist TOST, we conduct a Bayesian equivalence analysis using a paired $t$-test with Jeffreys prior~\cite{kruschke2013bayesian,kruschke2018rejecting}. The marginal posterior for the mean accuracy difference follows a $t$-distribution with $n{-}1$ degrees of freedom. For \cls{}--\cwm{}, the posterior probability within the $\pm$2\pp{} ROPE is $P(\text{ROPE}) = 0.975$, and the 95\% highest density interval (HDI $= [{+}0.10, {+}2.00]$\,pp) falls entirely inside the ROPE, confirming equivalence under both continuous and HDI-based decision criteria. For \cls{}--\traj{} at per-test level, $P(\text{ROPE}) = 0.542$ (inconclusive; 95\% HDI $= [{+}0.35, {+}3.52]$\,pp extends beyond the ROPE), consistent with the TOST inconclusive result after Holm correction ($p_{\text{adj}} = 0.075$). At variant level, \cls{}--\traj{} equivalence is supported by the Bayesian criterion ($P(\text{ROPE}) = 0.970$, HDI $= [{-}2.00, {+}1.03]$\,pp), but the frequentist TOST remains inconclusive after Holm-Bonferroni correction ($p_{\text{adj}} = 0.075$; Table~\ref{tab:holm}). This discrepancy reflects differing treatments of multiplicity: Bayesian ROPE evaluates each comparison independently, while Holm correction penalizes across all $k{=}5$ primary tests. For \cls{}--\cwm{}, both frameworks agree (TOST $p_{\text{adj}}{=}0.028$, $P(\text{ROPE}){=}0.975$); for \cls{}--\traj{} per-test, both are inconclusive. We conservatively follow the Holm-corrected TOST in the main text and report \cls{}--\traj{} equivalence as inconclusive.

109	\subsection{Function-Level Equivalence}
110	\label{sec:equivalence}
111	
112	We test pairwise equivalence on 523 variant-level instances across five seeds.\footnote{Seeds 789/0 use NF4 inference; the remaining three use bfloat16. Restricting to bfloat16 only: \cls{}--\cwm{} remains significant ($p{<}0.05$); \cls{}--\traj{} yields $p{=}0.128$.} Our Smallest Effect Size of Interest (SESOI) threshold of $\pm$2\pp{}~\cite{lakens2017,wellek2010} is motivated by domain considerations but was not pre-registered: at 85--88\% accuracy, 2\pp{} corresponds to ${\sim}$2--3 problems out of 114, below practitioner-relevant differences, and is consistent with between-seed variation (0.37--0.97\pp{} std). Within-problem predictions are correlated (ICC $= 0.191$, design effect [DEFF] $= 3.97$~\cite{moulton1990}); confidence intervals use clustered bootstrap (\S\ref{sec:setup}). Sensitivity analysis shows \cls{}--\cwm{} equivalence is robust across $\pm$1.5--3\pp{} margins (minimum $\pm$1.52\pp{}); \cls{}--\traj{} equivalence is margin-sensitive ($\pm$1.5\pp{}: $p{=}0.068$; Table~\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity}).
113	
114	\paragraph{\cls{}--\cwm{} equivalence.} The pooled gap is 1.24\pp{} (88.43\%--87.19\%); TOST confirms equivalence at $p = 0.007$ ($\pm$2\pp{} margin), surviving Holm-Bonferroni correction ($k{=}5$, adjusted $p{=}0.028$; Table~\ref{tab:holm}). Equivalence holds at margins as tight as $\pm$1.52\pp{} (Table~\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity}). On DeepSeek-Coder, the \cls{}--\cwm{} gap of 0.02\pp{} is consistent with equivalence but underpowered (2 seeds).
115	
116	\paragraph{\cls{}--\traj{} equivalence.} The pooled gap is $-$0.45\pp{} (95\% CI [$-$1.71, +0.84]); TOST yields $p = 0.025$ ($\pm$2\pp{}), nominally significant but not surviving Holm-Bonferroni correction ($k{=}5$, adjusted $p{=}0.075$; Table~\ref{tab:holm}). The DeepSeek \cls{}--\traj{} gap (2.69\pp{}) exceeds the $\pm$2\pp{} margin; \traj{} parity is model-dependent. This equivalence holds under their respective op

_source line 5784 · 2026-05-24T18:32:07.725417+00:00_

1	\begin{abstract}
2	Large Language Model (LLM)-based repair generates candidate patches, selecting the best via a verifier. Three execution-free alternatives to expensive execution-based verification have emerged: per-test binary classification (\cls{}), execution output generation (\cwm{}), and trajectory-level scoring (\traj{}). No prior work compares \cls{} and \cwm{} under matched conditions; the \traj{} comparison is inherently confounded.
3	
4	We present the first systematic comparison across two code LLM families (Qwen3, DeepSeek-Coder), two complexity levels, and multiple seeds under ${\leq}$8B Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) fine-tuning. At function level, \cls{} and \cwm{} are statistically equivalent across both families (Two One-Sided Tests [TOST] $p{=}0.007$, 5 seeds): classification is not inherently easier than generation when outputs are short. \traj{} equivalence is inconclusive after multiple comparison correction at current power (${\sim}$0.3), but the gap narrows from 3.11\pp{} (4B) to 1.07\pp{} (14B). At repository level, this equivalence diverges sharply (DeepSeek-Coder): the \cls{}--\cwm{} gap widens to 18--26\pp{} across 2--3 seeds under our ${\leq}$8B LoRA protocol, accompanied by format collapse. Preliminary 14B results (3 seeds, single family) suggest the gap may narrow at larger scale.
5	
6	Error complementarity and a failure mode taxonomy motivate complexity-aware routing, though simple heuristics capture $<$0.4\pp{} of oracle advantage.
7	\end{abstract}
8

_source line 5802 · 2026-05-25T05:50:16.394386+00:00_

5	
6	Despite active development along each axis, no prior work provides a systematic comparison holding model capacity, training data, and compute constant. Practitioners choosing a formulation for their verification pipeline must rely on results from different models, different datasets, and different evaluation protocols. Whether one formulation is uniformly preferable, or whether the choice depends on properties of the code being verified, remains an open empirical question. This connects to a broader pattern in NLP: task formulation---classification vs.\ generation vs.\ span extraction---systematically affects performance~\cite{raffel2020t5}, but the conditions under which each wins remain task-dependent and empirically unresolved. Recent evidence that generation-based verification outperforms classification in mathematical reasoning~\cite{hosseini2025genrm} raises a further question: does this advantage transfer to code verification, where outputs are longer and format-constrained? A related question is whether formulation choice matters at all relative to more basic decisions: at what complexity level does fine-tuning become necessary, and does formulation selection add value beyond what fine-tuning alone provides?
7	
8	We present the first systematic empirical comparison across two representative code LLM families (Qwen3, DeepSeek-Coder), two complexity levels (function-level and repository-level), and multiple seeds. Our contribution is primarily methodological: a matched experimental framework that controls for major confounding variables. The analysis also produces actionable outputs: a failure mode taxonomy that characterizes when and why each formulation fails (\S\ref{sec:repo}), error complementarity findings with practical ensemble implications (\S\ref{sec:analysis}), and formulation selection observations conditioned on code complexity (\S\ref{sec:conclusion}). The results suggest a three-layer structure. Fine-tuning is necessary (0-shot$\to$fine-tune

525	\section{Threats to Validity (Full Analysis)}
526	\label{app:threats}
527	
528	We identify five potential confounds and assess their impact on our conclusions.
529	
530	\paragraph{Checkpoint selection.} Val-based selection is unavailable for runs with NaN validation loss (DeepSeek \cwm{} repo-level runs, DeepSeek \cls{} s123), which default to the final epoch checkpoint. A checkpoint ablation on Qwen3-8B \cls{} s42 quantifies this confound: epoch~1 (ckpt-2200) achieves 85.34\%, val-selected (ckpt-3600) 84.48\%, and final epoch (ckpt-6633) 85.29\%---a $<$1\pp{} range. The val-selected checkpoint is the lowest, indicating no upward bias from checkpoint selection. The Qwen3-8B \traj{} comparison (epoch 2: 72.92\% vs.\ epoch 3: 70.83\%) shows a slightly larger ${\sim}$2\pp{} shift. For \cwm{}, the convergence analysis (Appendix~\ref{app:cwm_convergence}) shows eval loss at the final epoch is 1.8$\times$ its minimum; however, early-stopped checkpoints retain high unparseable rates, leaving the qualitative ranking unchanged.
531	
532	\paragraph{\cwm{} convergence analysis.}
533	\label{app:cwm_convergence}
534	Qwen3-8B \cwm{} repo-level eval loss reaches its minimum within ${\sim}$5\% of training (step 300--400 of 6{,}633) and increases monotonically thereafter, with no plateau or recovery (Table~\ref{tab:cwm_convergence}). This pattern is consistent across all three seeds, confirming systematic overfitting to trace generation rather than stochastic variation. The final-epoch eval loss is ${\sim}$1.8$\times$ the optimal value. By contrast, function-level \cwm{} achieves \cls{}-equivalent accuracy (TOST $p{=}0.007$) with the same training recipe, indicating that the overfit is specific to repo-level output complexity. 562	Despite these confounds, the repo-level \cls{}--\cwm{} gap (18--26\pp{}) exceeds any single assessed confound. Even under worst-case compounding (${\sim}$11.5\pp{}), the gap retains ${\geq}$7.5\pp{} margin; confounds predominantly disadvantage \cwm{}. However, correlated confounds and limited seed coverage mean definitive causal attribution requires multi-seed replication. At $n{=}48$, the minimum detectable effect (MDE) is ${\sim}$25\pp{}~\cite{hoenig2001}; the \cls{}--\cwm{} gap (29--35\pp{}) exceeds this threshold, while the \cls{}--\traj{} gap ($<$10\pp{}) falls below it, consistent with our TOST equivalence finding. Holm-Bonferroni correction across the five primary comparisons ($k{=}5$; Table~\ref{tab:holm}) confirms the \cls{}--\cwm{} gap (adjusted $p{=}0.015$) and function-level \cls{}--\cwm{} equivalence (adjusted $p{=}0.028$); function-level \cls{}--\traj{} equivalence does not survive correction (adjusted $p{=}0.075$), nor does the repo-level \cwm{}--\traj{} comparison (adjusted $p{=}0.078$). The consistent direction across both model families and all tested configurations supports a formulation-level explanation, though definitive causal attribution requires future experiments addressing each confound individually.
563

The \cwm{} 8B comparison uses 3 seeds per model. _source line 5828 · 2026-05-25T05:52:09.017272+00:00_

360	\label{app:extended_analysis}
361	
362	\paragraph{Function-level CWM metric robustness.}
363	At function level, \cwm{} outputs are short status strings (mean ${\sim}$5--15 tokens). The unparseable rate is only 1.39\%, and default-to-pass bias is $<$1\pp{}---well below the SESOI threshold. Exact string matching is appropriate for these short, structured outputs; semantic-but-format-different matches are rare given the constrained output space.
364	
365	\paragraph{(a) Error complementarity.}
366	\cls{} and \cwm{} are both FP-dominant at function level but at different rates, producing partial complementarity. Among \cls{} errors (seed 42), 56.5\% are false positives (PASS $\to$ FAIL); among \cwm{} errors, 70.3\% are false positives. Both formulations over-predict failure, but \cwm{}'s stronger FP bias means it incorrectly rejects passing tests that \cls{} correctly accepts. An oracle combination achieves 90.63\% accuracy (1713/1890 correct, 177 both-wrong; +2.80\pp{} over the best single formulation, \cls{} 87.83\%; single-seed estimate, seed 42). The complementarity arises from different \emph{degrees} of fail-bias rather than opposite error directions: \cwm{}'s generation bottleneck produces aggressive failure predictions, while \cls{}'s single-token output is more calibrated. Ensemble approaches exploiting this degree difference are a natural direction.
367	
368	\paragraph{(b) CWM error mode breakdown.}
369	Among unparseable \cwm{} outputs (Qwen3, repo-level), 76.9\% contain no execution keywords; 22.9\% contain ambiguous co-occurring pass/fail keywords. Format compliance varies sharply across repositories (scikit-learn: near-zero unparseable; sphinx: 100\%); repository-specific code patterns interact with the model's generation capabilities in complex ways. This per-repository variation is consistent with a model/tokenizer-specific failure rather than a formulation-level one.
370	
371	\paragraph{(c) Relaxed matching does not rescue \cwm{}.}
372	Relaxed matchin

41:The analysis above uses ICC $= 0.191$ from function-level analysis (\S\ref{sec:equivalence}), where mean cluster size $\bar{k} \approx 16.5$ yields DEFF $= 3.97$. At repository level, within-problem correlation is substantially stronger: ICC $= 0.3146$ with DEFF $= 114.4$ (\S\ref{sec:repo}), yielding an effective sample size of $n_{\text{eff}} \approx 9{,}784 / 114.4 \approx 86$ independent observations~\cite{moulton1990}---still sufficient for regression but limiting precision of variance component estimates. This is a consequence of the larger and more heterogeneous test suites in SWE-bench Verified problems. Under higher ICC, within-problem errors cluster more tightly and amplify the block-correlated per-test advantage at each $T$ stratum; the correction from i.i.d.\ to block-correlated grows monotonically with ICC. The function-level ICC $= 0.191$ used in Table~\ref{tab:oracle} thus provides a conservative lower bound on the per-test advantage. The repo-level correlation structure would yield an even larger gap between per-test and trajectory-level oracle resolve rates, which strengthens the case for per-test granularity at repository-level complexity.
372:Relaxed matching (tolerating format deviations, L0--L4) \emph{decreases} \cwm{} accuracy: 58.04\%$\to$56.98\% (seed 42) and 59.09\%$\to$55.91\% (seed 123). Unparseable predictions default to the majority class (pass, 72.96\%), which outperforms the model's own parseable predictions (38--43\%). The divergence widens from 26\pp{} to 27\pp{} under relaxed matching. This rules out the hypothesis that parsing strictness inflates the observed gap. Constrained decoding~\cite{willard2023outlines} could enforce format compliance but would not address the semantic accuracy deficit among parseable outputs. An accidental training artifact provides further evidence: a \cwm{} model inadvertently trained on functi

abstract.tex:2:Large Language Model (LLM)-based program repair generates candidate patches and selects the best via a verifier. Three execution-free alternatives to costly execution-based verification have emerged---per-test binary classification (cls{}), execution output generation (cwm{}), and trajectory-level scoring (traj{})---yet no prior work compares them under controlled conditions. We present the first systematic comparison across two code LLM families, two complexity levels, and multiple seeds under ${leq}$8B Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) fine-tuning. At function level, cls{} and cwm{} are statistically equivalent (Two One-Sided Tests [TOST] $p{=}0.007$, 5 seeds): classification offers no inherent advantage over generation when outputs are short. At repository level, however, this equivalence breaks down---cwm{} accuracy drops 18--26 percentage points below cls{} as format collapse emerges with longer, structured outputs. Preliminary 14B results narrow all gaps to ${sim}$1 percentage point, suggesting the divergence is capacity-contingent rather than formulation-inherent. Error complementarity across formulations motivates complexity-aware routing as a key open direction.

1	\begin{abstract}
2	Large Language Model (LLM)-based program repair generates candidate patches and selects the best via a verifier. Three execution-free alternatives to costly execution-based verification have emerged---per-test binary classification (\cls{}), execution output generation (\cwm{}), and trajectory-level scoring (\traj{})---yet no prior work compares them under controlled conditions. We present the first systematic comparison across two code LLM families, two complexity levels, and multiple seeds under ${\leq}$8B Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) fine-tuning. At function level, \cls{} and \cwm{} are statistically equivalent (Two One-Sided Tests [TOST] $p{=}0.007$, 5 seeds): classification offers no inherent advantage over generation when outputs are short. At repository level, however, this equivalence breaks down---\cwm{} accuracy drops 18--26 percentage points below \cls{} as format collapse emerges with longer, structured outputs. Preliminary 14B results narrow all gaps to ${\sim}$1 percentage point, suggesting the divergence is capacity-contingent rather than formulation-inherent. Error complementarity across formulations motivates complexity-aware routing as a key open direction.
3	\end{abstract}
4

diff --git a/docs/paper/abstract.tex b/docs/paper/abstract.tex
index 857f56d..78fadfa 100644
--- a/docs/paper/abstract.tex
+++ b/docs/paper/abstract.tex
@@ -1,7 +1,3 @@
 \begin{abstract}
-Large Language Model (LLM)-based repair generates candidate patches, selecting the best via a verifier. Three execution-free alternatives to expensive execution-based verification have emerged: per-test binary classification (\cls{}), execution output generation (\cwm{}), and trajectory-level scoring (\traj{}). No prior work compares \cls{} and \cwm{} under matched conditions; the \traj{} comparison is inherently confounded.
-
-We present the first systematic comparison across two code LLM families (Qwen3, DeepSeek-Coder), two complexity levels, and multiple seeds under ${\leq}$8B Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) fine-tuning. At function level, \cls{} and \cwm{} are statistically equivalent across both families (Two One-Sided Tests [TOST] $p{=}0.007$, 5 seeds): classification is not inherently easier than generation when outputs are short. \traj{} equivalence is inconclusive after multiple comparison correction at current power (${\sim}$0.3), but the gap narrows from 3.11\pp{} (4B) to 1.07\pp{} (14B). At repository level, this equivalence diverges sharply (DeepSeek-Coder): the \cls{}--\cwm{} gap widens to 18--26\pp{} across 2--3 seeds under our ${\leq}$8B LoRA protocol, accompanied by format collapse. Preliminary 14B results (3 seeds, single family) suggest the gap may narrow at larger scale.
-
-Error complementarity and a failure mode taxonomy motivate complexity-aware routing, though simple heuristics capture $<$0.4\pp{} of oracle advantage.
...
&lt;/persisted-output>

- TRAJ: #3A923A (forest green)
- Baseline: #9372B2 (灰紫)
- 背景白色，无网格或极浅网格，booktabs 风格

---

## Task 1: Figure 1 重画（PaperBanana）

**优先级最高。** 用 PaperBanana 重画 Figure 1 概念图，要求有高级感、专业感。

先 Read skill: `/home/ubuntu/agent-ml-research/skills/global/paper-banana.skill.md`

获取 API key: `request_secret(key_id="google_api_key", project="exec-sim-repair-verifier", purpose="PaperBanana Figure 1")`

**当前 Figure 1 内容**：展示三种 verification formulation 的对比：
- **CLS (左)**: 输入 code context + test → 输出 1 token (PASS/FAIL)，per-test 级别
- **CWM (中)**: 输入 code context + test → 生成 execution output string → exact match 判断 (O(L) tokens)
- **TRAJ (右)**: 输入 full patch + all tests → 输出 1 token (RESOLVED/UNRESOLVED)，patch 级别

**设计要求**：
- 三栏并列布局，每个 formulation 一栏
- 用统一色系（CLS 蓝、CWM 橙、TRAJ 绿）
- 展示 input → model → output 的流程
- 强调关键差异：output token 数量（1 vs L vs 1）、诊断粒度（per-test vs patch-level）
- 要有视觉层次感，不是简单的框+箭头
- 输出 PDF 格式，full-width（\textwidth）

当前文件：`docs/paper/figures/paper/fig1_reformulation.pdf`
替换这个文件。

---

## Task 2: 新增正文图表（draw-paper-figure）

先 Read skill: `/home/ubuntu/agent-ml-research/skills/global/draw-paper-figure.skill.md`

**Fig 3: Oracle-Stratified Performance（正文）**
已有文件 `docs/paper/figures/paper/fig3_oracle_stratified.pdf`——如果存在，检查其内容和色系是否与统一色系一致。如果需要重画或色系不一致，重画。

如果不存在，新画一个：
- 分组柱状图，展示 CLS/CWM/TRAJ 在 repo-level 的 accuracy 对比
- 突出 18-26pp gap
- 从 experiments.tex Table 3 提取数据
- 单栏宽度 (\columnwidth)

---

## Task 3: 新增附录图表（draw-paper-figure）

以下附录图表全部新建，用统一色系：

**A. CWM Unparseable Rate 可视化**
- 柱状图或折线图，展示 CWM 在 function vs repo level 的 unparseable rate
- 从论文数据：function ~0%, repo 53-74%
- 视觉上突出 format collapse 的严重性

**B. TOST Sensitivity Analysis**
- 折线图，x 轴 = equivalence margin (1.0-3.0 pp)，y 轴 = TOST p-value
- 两条线：CLS-CWM 和 CLS-TRAJ
- 标注 α=0.05 水平线
- 数据在 appendix.tex Table (TOST sensitivity) 中

**C. Per-Mutation Type Accuracy Heatmap**
- 热力图，行 = mutation type，列 = CLS/CWM/TRAJ
- 数据在 appendix 的 per-mutation breakdown 中

**每张图输出 PDF，保存到 `docs/paper/figures/paper/`**

16	
17	# Paper Banana — 高品质学术概念图生成
18	
19	PaperBanana 由 Gemini Pro 自动写 matplotlib 代码，VLM Critic 评估渲染结果并迭代优化，输出顶会级别 300 DPI 概念图。
20	
21	**质量底线：每张图必须达到顶会论文的美观度——高级感、专业感、精致感。绝不接受 matplotlib 默认样式的廉价感。**
22	
23	## 适用范围
24	
25	| 场景 | 用什么 |
26	|------|--------|
27	| **概念图**（架构图、流程图、framework 示意） | **PaperBanana** ← 本 skill |
28	| **统计图**（scatter、bar、line、heatmap、forest） | **draw-paper-figure.skill.md**。**不要用 PaperBanana 画统计图** |
29	| 离线 / 无 GOOGLE_API_KEY | **draw-paper-figure.skill.md** |
30	
31	## 执行环境
32	
33	**PaperBanana 在本机（EC2）运行，不要 SSH 到远程服务器。**
34	
35	EC2（ap-northeast-1 东京）可直接访问 Gemini API，无需代理。
36

39	### Step 1：获取 GOOGLE_API_KEY
40	
41	```python
42	request_secret(key_id="google_api_key", project="&lt;project_name>", purpose="PaperBanana 概念图生成")
43	# 等待 Director 审批后
44	poll_secret_request(request_id="<返回的 request_id>", project="&lt;project_name>")
45	```
46	
47	如果密钥尚未存储，告知 Director 需要 GOOGLE_API_KEY。
48	
49	### Step 2：安装验证（每次使用前必须检查）
50	
51	```bash
52	# 检查是否已安装
53	paperbanana --version 2>/dev/null || {
54	  echo "PaperBanana 未安装，正在安装..."
55	  pip install paperbanana -q
56	  paperbanana --version
57	}
58	```
59	
60	如果 `paperbanana --version` 失败（命令不存在），必须先 `pip install paperbanana -q` 安装。版本需 ≥ 0.1.2。

62	**设计前必读** `skills/global/paper-visualization.skill.md` 的概念图 L1（防丑 7 条）+ L2（叙事 6 条）+ L3 Pattern + Checklist。统计图部分不适用于 PaperBanana。

64	## 命令：generate

66	概念图用 `generate` 子命令。**必须显式指定 `--vlm-model gemini-2.5-pro`**（默认模型 `gemini-2.0-flash` 已废弃，不指定会崩溃）。**`--iterations 5`**。

19	**质量底线：每张图必须达到 NeurIPS/ICML/EMNLP best paper 的视觉水准——高级感、专业感、精致感。绝不接受 matplotlib 默认样式的廉价感（灰背景、粗网格线、tab10 色板、默认蓝色）。**
20	
21	**生成图片后必须 Read PNG 做视觉检查，至少迭代 3 轮（Hero Figure 至少 5 轮）。第一版画出来基本不能直接用。**
22	
23	本 skill 提供：
24	- 通用样式（rcParams + 色板）
25	- 三个常用图表的起点模板（reliability/heatmap/scatter）
26	- NeurIPS 经验提炼的 pattern 实现示例
27	
28	## 输入字段（从 Paper Agent 传入）
29	
30	- `fig_id` / `type` / `placement` / `data_source` / `aspect_ratio` / 输出路径
31	- **`narrative_intent`**：一句话，这张图要让读者相信什么
32	- **`design_notes`**：从 paper-visualization L3 选的 pattern 列表
33	
34	## 画图工作流
35	
36	1. 读 paper-visualization skill 的 L1 防丑原则 + L2 叙事原则
37	2. 根据 `narrative_intent` 选起点模板（本 skill §模板）
38	3. 按 `design_notes` 中每个 pattern 添加相应技法（本 skill §pattern 实现）
39	4. 提交前过本 skill §checklist
40

1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	We systematically compare \cls{}, \cwm{}, and \traj{} across two representative code LLM families, two complexity levels, and multiple seeds to characterize the formulation landscape for neural code verification.
5	
6	\subsection{Experimental Setup}
7	\label{sec:setup}
8	
9	\paragraph{Datasets.}
10	\emph{Function-level}: mutated variants from HumanEval~\cite{chen2021humaneval} and MBPP~\cite{austin2021mbpp} (five mutation types), labels verified by execution. 19,076 samples (train 15,290 / val 1,896 / test 1,890; pass:fail $\approx$ 52:48, majority baseline 51.75\%).
11	\emph{Repo-level}: per-test instances from SWE-bench Verified~\cite{chowdhury2024swebenchverified} mutants. ${\sim}$70K training samples; val ($n{=}9{,}335$) and test ($n{=}9{,}784$) have zero problem overlap (majority baseline 72.96\%). Val-based checkpoint selection follows standard practice~\cite{jimenez2024swebench}; runs with NaN validation loss (due to truncated targets exceeding 2,048-token limits) default to the final epoch checkpoint (Appendix~\ref{app:hyperparams}). Qwen3 \cwm{} runs show early overfitting (best at ${\sim}$6\% of training); checkpoint details in Appendix~\ref{app:hyperparams}.
12	
13	\paragraph{Models and training.}
14	We fine-tune Qwen3-4B and Qwen3-8B~\cite{qwen2025qwen3} with Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA)~\cite{hu2022lora} (rank 16, 3 epochs) across five seeds. Seeds 42/123/456 use bfloat16; seeds 789/0 use NormalFloat 4-bit (NF4) quantization~\cite{dettmers2024qlora} to test robustness to precision reduction (function-level accuracy remains within seed noise across both precisions; see footnote in \S\ref{sec:equivalence}). As a cross-model consistency check, we additionally fine-tune DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B~\cite{guo2024deepseekcoder} with identical LoRA configuration (2 seeds each for \cls{} and \cwm{}). Our \cwm{} baseline is a LoRA fine-tuned variant for matched comparison, not a reproduction of the 32B Code World Model

{
  "file_path": "/tmp/gen_fig_tost_sensitivity.py",
  "content": "import matplotlib\nmatplotlib.use('Agg')\nimport matplotlib.pyplot as plt\nimport numpy as np\nfrom scipy import stats\n\nplt.rcParams.update({\n    'font.family': 'DejaVu Sans',\n    'font.size': 11,\n    'axes.titlesize': 13,\n    'axes.labelsize': 12,\n    'xtick.labelsize': 10,\n    'ytick.labelsize': 10,\n    'legend.fontsize': 10,\n    'figure.dpi': 300,\n    'savefig.dpi': 300,\n    'savefig.bbox': 'tight',\n    'savefig.pad_inches': 0.05,\n    'axes.spines.top': False,\n    'axes.spines.right': False,\n})\n\nCLS_COLOR = '#3274A1'\nTRAJ_COLOR = '#3A923A'\n\n# CLS-CWM: known data points from Table\ncls_cwm_margins = [1.0, 1.5, 1.52, 1.71, 2.0, 3.0, 5.0]\ncls_cwm_pvals = [0.50, 0.054, 0.05, 0.027, 0.007, 4.6e-5, 1e-7]\n\n# CLS-TRAJ: compute from known parameters\n# gap = -0.45pp (CLS higher), df=4, seed-level paired\n# At margin=2.0: TOST p=0.025 (binding constraint is p_lower)\n# At margin=1.5: p=0.068\ncls_traj_gap = -0.45\ncls_traj_df = 4\n\n# Derive SE from margin=2.0, p=0.025\n# p_lower = P(T > t_lower | df) where t_lower = (gap + margin)/SE\n# The binding constraint at margin=2.0 is p_lower = 0.025\nt_crit = stats.t.ppf(1 - 0.025, cls_traj_df)  # ~2.132\ncls_traj_SE = (cls_traj_gap + 2.0) / t_crit  # 1.55 / 2.132\n\ndef tost_p(gap, margin, SE, df):\n    t_upper = (margin - gap) / SE\n    t_lower = (gap + margin) / SE\n    p_upper = stats.t.cdf(-t_upper, df)\n    p_lower = 1 - stats.t.cdf(t_lower, df)\n    return max(p_upper, p_lower)\n\n# Verify: at margin=2.0 should give ~0.025\np_check = tost_p(cls_traj_gap, 2.0, cls_traj_SE, cls_traj_df)\nprint(f\"CLS-TRAJ verification at margin=2.0: p={p_check:.4f} (expect ~0.025)\")\n\n# Generate smooth curve for CLS-TRAJ\nmargins_smooth = np.linspace(0.5, 5.0, 100)\ncls_traj_pvals_smooth = [tost_p(cls_traj_gap, m, cls_traj_SE, cls_traj_df) for m in margins_smooth]\n\n# Also verify at margin=1.5\np_15 = tost_p(cls_traj_gap, 1.5, cls_traj_SE, cls_traj_df)\nprint(f\"CLS-TRAJ at margin=1.5: p={p_15:.4f} (expect ~0.068)\")\n\nfig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(5, 3.5))\n\n# CLS-CWM line\nax.plot(cls_cwm_margins, cls_cwm_pvals, 'o-', color=CLS_COLOR, linewidth=2,\n        markersize=6, markerfacecolor='white', markeredgecolor=CLS_COLOR,\n        markeredgewidth=1.5, label='CLS–CWM', zorder=3)\n\n# CLS-TRAJ line (smooth)\nax.plot(margins_smooth, cls_traj_pvals_smooth, '-', color=TRAJ_COLOR, linewidth=2,\n        zorder=2, label='CLS–TRAJ')\n# Add known data points\nax.plot([1.5, 2.0], [0.068, 0.025], 'o', color=TRAJ_COLOR, markersize=6,\n        markerfacecolor='white', markeredgecolor=TRAJ_COLOR,\n        markeredgewidth=1.5, zorder=4)\n\n# Alpha threshold\nax.axhline(y=0.05, color='#CC0000', linestyle='--', linewidth=1.0, alpha=0.7, zorder=1)\nax.text(4.85, 0.055, 'α = 0.05', fontsize=9, color='#CC0000', ha='right', va='bottom')\n\n# Holm-corrected threshold for k=5\nax.axhline(y=0.025, color='#CC0000', linestyle=':', linewidth=0.8, alpha=0.5, zorder=1)\nax.text(4.85, 0.027, 'α/2 (Holm)', fontsize=8, color='#CC0000', ha='right', va='bottom', alpha=0.7)\n\n# Annotate minimum equivalence margin for CLS-CWM\nax.annotate('±1.52pp\\n(min. equiv.)', xy=(1.52, 0.05),\n            xytext=(2.2, 0.20), fontsize=8, color=CLS_COLOR,\n            ha='center',\n            arrowprops=dict(arrowstyle='->', color=CLS_COLOR, lw=1.0))\n\nax.set_xlabel('Equivalence Margin (±pp)')\nax.set_ylabel('TOST p-value')\nax.set_yscale('log')\nax.set_ylim(1e-7, 1)\nax.set_xlim(0.8, 5.2)\nax.legend(loc='upper right', framealpha=0.9, edgecolor='#DDDDDD')\nax.grid(axis='both', alpha=0.15, zorder=0)\n\nplt.tight_layout()\noutpath = '/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_16/projects/exec-sim-repair-verifier/docs/paper/figures/paper/fig_tost_sensitivity.pdf'\nplt.savefig(outpath)\nplt.savefig(outpath.replace('.pdf', '.png'))\nplt.close()\nprint(f'Saved: {outpath}')\n"
}

linestyle='--', linewidth=1.0, alpha=0.7, zorder=1)\nax.text(4.85, 0.055, 'α = 0.05', fontsize=9, color='#CC0000', ha='right', va='bottom')\n\n# Holm-corrected threshold for k=5\nax.axhline(y=0.025, color='#CC0000', linestyle=':', linewidth=0.8, alpha=0.5, zorder=1)\nax.text(4.85, 0.027, 'α/2 (Holm)', fontsize=8, color='#CC0000', ha='right', va='bottom', alpha=0.7)\n\n# Annotate minimum equivalence margin for CLS-CWM\nax.annotate('±1.52pp\\n(min. equiv.)', xy=(1.52, 0.05),\n            xytext=(2.2, 0.20), fontsize=8, color=CLS_COLOR,\n            ha='center',\n            arrowprops=dict(arrowstyle='->', color=CLS_COLOR, lw=1.0))\n\nax.set_xlabel('Equivalence Margin (±pp)')\nax.set_ylabel('TOST p-value')\nax.set_yscale('log')\nax.set_ylim(1e-7, 1)\nax.set_xlim(0.8, 5.2)\nax.legend(loc='upper right', framealpha=0.9, edgecolor='#DDDDDD')\nax.grid(axis='both', alpha=0.15, zorder=0)\n\nplt.tight_layout()\noutpath = '/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_16/projects/exec-sim-repair-verifier/docs/paper/figures/paper/fig_tost_sensitivity.pdf'\nplt.savefig(outpath)\nplt.savefig(outpath.replace('.pdf', '.png'))\nplt.close()\nprint(f'Saved: {outpath}')\n"
  }
}

410	\section{TOST Margin Sensitivity}
411	\label{app:tost_sensitivity}
412	
413	Table~\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity} reports the TOST equivalence $p$-value as a function of the equivalence margin, computed on the 5-seed variant-level \cls{}--\cwm{} comparison (gap $= 1.24$\pp{}, 523 variants, 114 clusters, clustered bootstrap).
414	
415	\begin{table}[h]
416	\centering
417	\small
418	\caption{TOST margin sensitivity for \cls{}--\cwm{} variant-level equivalence (5 seeds, 523 variants, 114 clusters). The smallest margin achieving equivalence at $\alpha = 0.05$ is $\pm$1.52\pp{}.}
419	\label{tab:tost_sensitivity}
420	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}
421	\begin{tabular}{ccc}
422	\toprule
423	\textbf{Margin ($\pm$\pp{})} & \textbf{$p$-value} & \textbf{Equivalent ($\alpha{=}0.05$)?} \\
424	\midrule
425	1.0 & $\gg$0.05 & No \\
426	1.5 & 0.054 & No (borderline) \\
427	\textbf{1.52} & ${\sim}$\textbf{0.05} & \textbf{Smallest margin} \\
428	1.71 & 0.027 & Yes \\
429	2.0 & 0.007 & Yes \\
430	3.0 & $4.6{\times}10^{-5}$ & Yes \\
431	5.0 & $\ll$0.001 & Yes \\
432	\bottomrule
433	\end{tabular}
434	\end{table}
435	
436	The minimum equivalence margin is $\pm$1.52\pp{}, 0.48\pp{} below our pre-specified $\pm$2\pp{} threshold. The $\pm$2\pp{} threshold was set based on deployment relevance rather than observed data: at function-level accuracy (85--88\%), a 2\pp{} per-test difference corresponds to ${\sim}$38 additional errors across 1,890 tests (${\sim}$2--3 problems out of 114), comparable in magnitude to between-seed standard deviation (0.37--0.97\pp{}) and checkpoint selection noise ($<$1\pp{}). Differences within this range do not reliably distinguish formulations from training noise in pipeline selection decisions. For \cls{}--\traj{}, the TOST at $\pm$2\pp{} yields $p{=}0.025$, nominally significant but not surviving Holm-Bonferroni correction at $k{=}5$ (adjusted $p{=}0.075$; Table~\ref{tab:holm}). _source line 5954 · 2026-05-25T07:09:32.450375+00:00_

518	
519	\section{CWM Parseable Bias Analysis}
520	\label{app:parseable_bias}
521	
522	The default-to-pass assignment for unparseable \cwm{} outputs is not random with respect to instance difficulty. Unparseable instances have significantly higher pass rates than parseable ones (74.6\% vs.\ 68.3\%, $\chi^2$ $p = 5.9 \times 10^{-10}$), and \cls{} models achieve higher accuracy on unparseable instances (86.4\% vs.\ 79.1\%, $p = 3.3 \times 10^{-18}$). This indicates that \cwm{}'s parsing failures concentrate on simpler, pass-heavy instances where the majority-class default happens to be correct more often. Cross-model Jaccard overlap of unparseable instance sets is 0.46, confirming this pattern is instance-driven (specific inputs consistently cause parsing failure across models) rather than stochastic. The net effect is that \cwm{}'s reported accuracy is inflated by default-correct assignments on easy instances, while its effective discriminative accuracy on harder instances is substantially lower than the aggregate figure suggests.
523	
524	
525	\section{Threats to Validity (Full Analysis)}
526	\label{app:threats}
527

Parseable-only accuracy (inside bars) decreases from 4B to 8B despite rising overall accuracy, confirming default-assignment inflation.}\n\\label{fig:cwm_unparseable}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe default-to-pass assignment for unparseable \\cwm{} outputs is not random with respect to instance difficulty."
}
```

_source line 6010 · 2026-05-25T07:22:43.157490+00:00_

[Director 指令] [Director 指令] **论文结构 + 写作修改（用户反馈，紧急）**

用户明确反馈 5 点，全部必须执行：

## 1. Abstract 重写——不要像实验报告
当前 abstract 太数据堆砌（TOST p=0.007、18-26pp 等具体数字罗列）。重写要求：
- 叙事驱动：问题→为什么重要→我们做了什么→核心发现→意义
- 不要堆数字，最多保留 1-2 个关键数字来说明 finding 的 magnitude
- 语感参考顶会好论文的 abstract：一个自然的 story，不是列指标
- 仍然 ≤ 200 words，单段
- 先 Read `skills/global/paper-writing-norms.skill.md` 学习写作规范

## 2. Introduction 缩短——不要像实验报告
当前 intro 第 3 段（"We present the first..."）太长太密，像实验结果摘要。修改要求：
- 砍掉 intro 中的大段数字预览（35-36pp、p=0.007、z=-23.7、18-26pp 等）
- Intro 只给 high-level insight，具体数字留给 Experiments
- Contributions list 精简：每条 1-2 句话说 what + why it matters，不要嵌套数字
- 目标：Intro 总长度减少 30-40%

## 3. Related Work 移到 Section 2
在 `main.tex` 中，把 `\input{related_work}` 从当前位置（experiments 和 conclusion 之间）移到 `\input{introduction}` 之后、`\input{method}` 之前。
- Related Work 成为 Section 2
- Method 变成 Section 3
- Experiments 变成 Section 4
- Conclusion 变成 Section 5
- **检查并更新所有 .tex 文件中的 cross-reference**：如果有 hardcoded 的 §2、§3 等引用需要更新。但 \ref{} 引用会自动更新，不用改。

## 4. 修复行号
检查 ACL review 模板的 `\usepackage[review]{acl}` 是否正确启用了行号。如果行号显示有问题，修复。

## 5. 确保正文 ≤ 8 页
以上修改后编译，Conclusion 必须在第 8 页内结束。如果仍超页：
- 优先压缩 Related Work（这是最容易压缩的 section）
- 其次检查 Figure 1 的 caption 是否可以精简
- 最后考虑压缩 Experiments 中的冗余描述

**完成后**：编译验证页数 + commit。

1	---
2	name: paper-writing-norms
3	description: 顶会级别论文写作风格规范——章节写法（Abstract/Intro/Related Work）、去 AI 风格、视觉密度、叙事哲学。Paper Agent 和 Worker 写论文章节时必须加载。
4	allowed-tools: [Read, Write, Edit, Glob, Grep]
5	scope: global
6	version: 2
7	priority: 9

9	  - writing

16	  - paper_agent
17	---
18	
19	# 论文写作风格规范
20	
21	> 核心哲学：**论文是一个有说服力的故事，不是实验报告。** 审稿人在几十秒内判断论文价值——如果 Abstract 和 Introduction 读起来像实验日志，论文就已经失败了。
22	
23	---
24	
25	## 一、总体写作哲学
26	
27	### 1.1 故事驱动，不是数据堆砌
28	
29	- 论文必须以"故事线"为核心展开：问题 → 洞察 → 方法 → 验证
30	- 禁止大量实验数据堆砌——每个数字出现必须服务于叙事推进
31	- 附录内容必须在正文中有明确引用且融入叙事，禁止简单堆砌
32	- 如果附录数据对核心叙事有重大影响，根据附录数据调整论文 framing
33	
34	### 1.2 主线统一性
35	
36	- 当 idea 在研究过程中发生变化后，**必须重审全文逻辑**
37	- 常见问题：Introduction 讲的故事 A，Method 做的是故事 B，Results 又在讲故事 C
38	- 修复方式：从最终实验结论反推，重写 Introduction 的问题定义和动机
39	- 每次 idea 调整后检查：Abstract → Introduction → Method → Results → Discussion → Conclusion 是否形成一条连贯主线
40	
41	### 1.3 专有名词必须有定义
42	
43	- 首次出现的专有名词/缩写必须给出完整定义和解释
44	- Abstract 中使用的缩写也必须在 Abstract 内定义（如 LLM = Large Language Model）
45	- 不能假设读者知道你自己定义的术语——论文从来没解释过的概念不能直接使用
46	- 通用规则：如果一个非标准术语第一次出现时没有括号定义，就是写作 bug
47	
48	### 1.4 写作前必读同类优秀论文
49	
50	- 每个章节动笔前，找 **2-3 篇目标会议近 1-2 年的高分论文**作为风格参考——同类问题或同类方法皆可
51	- 重点学：
52	  - Abstract 怎么用最少篇幅讲完故事
53	  - Introduction 怎么从大问题切入到具体贡献
54	  - Related Work 怎么 positioning 自己（不是 survey）
55	  - Table/Figure 怎么压缩信息密度
56	- **不是抄袭**，是学习同行的故事节奏和审美——不同子领域叙事节奏差异很大
57	- 实操：Paper Agent 可以从 `literature_map.yaml` 中挑 `role=foundation` 或 `role=direct-competitor` 的论文，把 `{title, venue, year, abstract}` 附在 Worker 的 task_prompt 中作为概念参考；如需学习写作风格本身，让 Worker 通过 WebSearch 取得论文链接并 fetch 全文
58	
59	---
60	
61	## 二、Abstract 写作规范
62	
63	### 2.1 四个必答问题（150-250 words）
64	
65	Abstract 必须在很短的篇幅内清楚回答：
66	1. **研究什么问题** — 1-2 句话交代背景和痛点
67	2. **为什么重要** — 现有方法的不足（不用写太多）
68	3. **本文提出什么** — 核心贡献的关键机制（简要，不堆砌细节）
69	4. **结果说明什么** — 用具体实验结论支撑贡献
70	
71	### 2.2 禁忌
72	
73	- **禁止堆砌数据**：Abstract 不是 Results 的缩写。最多引用 2-3 个关键数字，要服务于 claim
74	- **
…[truncated 5709 chars]

1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	LLM-based automated program repair systems increasingly rely on test-time compute scaling: generating $K$ candidate patches and selecting the best via a verifier~\cite{snell2024scaling}. Full execution verification is reliable but expensive (\$1--3 per candidate at repository scale\footnote{Based on per-instance costs reported by \citet{yang2024sweagent} and \citet{xia2024agentless}.}), driving demand for execution-free alternatives. Three distinct formulations have emerged for this task: \textbf{generation-based simulation}~\cite{meta2025cwm,maimon2026selfexecution} predicts execution output strings per test; \textbf{trajectory-level scoring}~\cite{shum2025swerm} predicts a binary patch verdict without per-test decomposition; and \textbf{per-test classification}~\cite{ni2023lever,chen2023codet} predicts a pass/fail token per test. Each makes different tradeoffs between diagnostic granularity, output complexity, and inference cost.
5	
6	Despite active development along each axis, no prior work provides a systematic comparison holding model capacity, training data, and compute constant. Practitioners choosing a formulation for their verification pipeline must rely on results from different models, different datasets, and different evaluation protocols. Whether one formulation is uniformly preferable, or whether the choice depends on properties of the code being verified, remains an open empirical question. This connects to a broader pattern in NLP: task formulation (classification vs.\ generation vs.\ span extraction) systematically affects performance~\cite{raffel2020t5}, but the conditions under which each wins remain task-dependent and empirically unresolved. Recent evidence that generation-based verification outperforms classification in mathematical reasoning~\cite{hosseini2025genrm} raises a further question: does this advantage transfer to code verification, where outputs are longer and format-constraine

_source line 6030 · 2026-05-25T07:24:12.354691+00:00_

1	\begin{abstract}
2	Large Language Model (LLM)-based program repair generates candidate patches and selects the best via a verifier. Three execution-free alternatives to costly execution-based verification have emerged, namely per-test binary classification (\cls{}), execution output generation (\cwm{}), and trajectory-level scoring (\traj{}), yet no prior work compares them under controlled conditions. We present the first systematic comparison across two code LLM families, two complexity levels, and multiple seeds under ${\leq}$8B Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) fine-tuning. At function level, \cls{} and \cwm{} are statistically equivalent (Two One-Sided Tests [TOST] $p{=}0.007$, 5 seeds): classification offers no inherent advantage over generation when outputs are short. At repository level, however, this equivalence breaks down: \cwm{} accuracy drops 18--26 percentage points below \cls{} as format collapse emerges with longer, structured outputs. Preliminary 14B results narrow all gaps to ${\sim}$1 percentage point, suggesting the divergence is capacity-contingent rather than formulation-inherent. Error complementarity across formulations motivates complexity-aware routing as a key open direction.
3	\end{abstract}
4

{
  "replace_all": "False",

  "old_string": "\\begin{abstract}\nLarge Language Model (LLM)-based program repair generates candidate patches and selects the best via a verifier. Three execution-free alternatives to costly execution-based verification have emerged, namely per-test binary classification (\\cls{}), execution output generation (\\cwm{}), and trajectory-level scoring (\\traj{}), yet no prior work compares them under controlled conditions. We present the first systematic comparison across two code LLM families, two complexity levels, and multiple seeds under ${\\leq}$8B Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) fine-tuning. At function level, \\cls{} and \\cwm{} are statistically equivalent (Two One-Sided Tests [TOST] $p{=}0.007$, 5 seeds): classification offers no inherent advantage over generation when outputs are short. At repository level, however, this equivalence breaks down: \\cwm{} accuracy drops 18--26 percentage points below \\cls{} as format collapse emerges with longer, structured outputs. Preliminary 14B results narrow all gaps to ${\\sim}$1 percentage point, suggesting the divergence is capacity-contingent rather than formulation-inherent. Error complementarity across formulations motivates complexity-aware routing as a key open direction.\n\\end{abstract}",
  "new_string": "\\begin{abstract}\nExecution-free verification promises to replace costly test execution in Large Language Model (LLM)-based program repair, yet practitioners must choose among three fundamentally different formulations with no controlled comparison to guide the decision: per-test classification (\\cls{}), execution output generation (\\cwm{}), and trajectory-level scoring (\\traj{}). We present the first systematic comparison, controlling for model capacity, training data, and evaluation protocol across two code LLM families (Qwen3, DeepSeek-Coder), two complexity levels, and multiple random seeds under ${\\leq}$8B Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) fine-tuning. Our central finding is that \\emph{code complexity moderates the classification--generation tradeoff}. When outputs are short (function-level verification), all three formulations achieve statistically equivalent accuracy. When outputs grow longer and more structured (repository-level verification), this equivalence breaks down: generation-based verification suffers format collapse, falling well below classification. Preliminary results at 14B suggest this divergence narrows with scale, pointing to a capacity-dependent rather than formulation-inherent limitation. A failure mode taxonomy and error complementarity analysis provide actionable guidance for formulation selection and motivate complexity-aware routing as a key open direction.\n\\end{abstract}"
}
```

{
  "replace_all": "False",
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_16/projects/exec-sim-repair-verifier/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
  "old_string": "We present the first systematic empirical comparison across two representative code LLM families (Qwen3, DeepSeek-Coder), two complexity levels (function-level and repository-level), and multiple seeds. Our contribution is primarily methodological: a matched experimental framework that controls for major confounding variables. The analysis also produces actionable outputs: a failure mode taxonomy that characterizes when and why each formulation fails (\\S\\ref{sec:repo}), error complementarity findings with practical ensemble implications (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}), and formulation selection observations conditioned on code complexity (\\S\\ref{sec:conclusion}). The results suggest a three-layer structure. Fine-tuning is necessary (0-shot$\\to$fine-tuned gains of 35--36 percentage points (pp) at repo level). After fine-tuning under our ${\\leq}$8B LoRA protocol, \\emph{formulation choice is moderated by code complexity, operating through output format}: function-level outputs average ${\\sim}$5--15 tokens (minimizing generation-specific failures per \\S\\ref{sec:failure-modes}), while repo-level outputs average ${\\sim}$477 tokens (triggering format collapse): at function level, all formulations achieve comparable accuracy, consistent with the theoretical analysis in \\S\\ref{sec:failure-modes}, which predicts that short outputs minimize generation-specific failures. At repository level under our ${\\leq}$8B LoRA protocol, this equivalence diverges; variant-level analysis identifies three distinct failure modes consistent across model families, though gap magnitude depends on multiple confounds (\\S\\ref{sec:threats}). Figure~\\ref{fig:reformulation} illustrates the three formulations.\n\nWe make the following contributions:\n\n\\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt]\n\\item \\textbf{Complexity-moderated formulation choice.} We establish a three-layer structure: (1)~fine-tuning is necessary (35--36\\pp{} gains); (2)~at function level, formulations are near-equivalent: \\cls{} and \\cwm{} achieve statistical equivalence (TOST $p{=}0.007$, Holm-corrected, Qwen3 5 seeds; DeepSeek directionally consistent, 2 seeds), while \\traj{} equivalence is inconclusive after Holm correction, but the gap narrows from 3.11\\pp{} at 4B to 1.07\\pp{} at 14B (\\S\\ref{sec:scaling}); (3)~at repository level under ${\\leq}$8B LoRA, this equivalence diverges ($z{=}-23.7$, DeepSeek-Coder), with \\cwm{} falling 18--26\\pp{} below \\cls{}, accompanied by format collapse; four interventions all fail to close the gap. The TOST framework demonstrates strict equivalence rather than mere non-significance. Error analysis reveals partially complementary patterns (oracle: 90.63\\% function, 95.83\\% repo), though simple heuristics capture $<$0.4\\pp{}, framing ensemble routing as an open challenge (\\S\\ref{sec:equivalence}, \\S\\ref{sec:repo}, \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Failure mode taxonomy.} Variant-level analysis identifies three distinct failure modes at repository level, namely format collapse (\\cwm{}), decision collapse (\\cls{} under truncate preprocessing), and aggregation penalty (\\cls{} under all-pass rule), providing actionable diagnostic criteria for practitioners selecting a verification formulation (\\S\\ref{sec:repo}, Table~\\ref{tab:taxonomy}).\n\\end{itemize}",
  "new_string": "We present the first systematic comparison across two code LLM families (Qwen3, DeepSeek-Coder), two complexity levels, and multiple seeds under ${\\leq}$8B LoRA fine-tuning. By controlling model capacity, training data, and evaluation protocol, we isolate the effect of formulation choice itself. Our central finding is that \\emph{code complexity moderates the classification--generation tradeoff}: at function level, all formulations achieve comparable accuracy; at repository level, this equivalence breaks down as generation-based verification suffers format collapse. Figure~\\ref{fig:reformulation} illustrates the three formulations.\n\nWe make the following contributions:\n\n\\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt]\n\\item \\textbf{Complexity-moderated equivalence and divergence.} At function level, classification and generation are statistically equivalent; at repository level, generation accuracy drops sharply due to output format complexity, while classification and trajectory scoring remain competitive (\\S\\ref{sec:equivalence}, \\S\\ref{sec:repo}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Failure mode taxonomy and error analysis.} We identify three distinct failure modes at repository level (format collapse, decision collapse, aggregation penalty) and show that formulations make partially complementary errors, motivating complexity-aware routing (\\S\\ref{sec:repo}, \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\\end{itemize}"
}

_source line 6038 · 2026-05-25T07:25:12.310239+00:00_

1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	LLM-based automated program repair systems increasingly rely on test-time compute scaling: generating $K$ candidate patches and selecting the best via a verifier~\cite{snell2024scaling}. Full execution verification is reliable but expensive (\$1--3 per candidate at repository scale\footnote{Based on per-instance costs reported by \citet{yang2024sweagent} and \citet{xia2024agentless}.}), driving demand for execution-free alternatives. Three distinct formulations have emerged for this task: \textbf{generation-based simulation}~\cite{meta2025cwm,maimon2026selfexecution} predicts execution output strings per test; \textbf{trajectory-level scoring}~\cite{shum2025swerm} predicts a binary patch verdict without per-test decomposition; and \textbf{per-test classification}~\cite{ni2023lever,chen2023codet} predicts a pass/fail token per test. Each makes different tradeoffs between diagnostic granularity, output complexity, and inference cost.
5	
6	Despite active development along each axis, no prior work provides a systematic comparison holding model capacity, training data, and compute constant. Practitioners choosing a formulation for their verification pipeline must rely on results from different models, different datasets, and different evaluation protocols. Whether one formulation is uniformly preferable, or whether the choice depends on properties of the code being verified, remains an open empirical question. This connects to a broader pattern in NLP: task formulation (classification vs.\ generation vs.\ span extraction) systematically affects performance~\cite{raffel2020t5}, but the conditions under which each wins remain task-dependent and empirically unresolved. Recent evidence that generation-based verification outperforms classification in mathematical reasoning~\cite{hosseini2025genrm} raises a further question: does this advantage transfer to code verification, where outputs are longer and format-constraine
…[truncated 1665 chars]

_source line 6058 · 2026-05-25T07:32:56.132548+00:00_

[Director 指令] [Director 指令] **两个排版修复**

## 1. 行号修复
用户说行号不对。ACL review 模板的行号应该是**每一行连续编号**（1, 2, 3, 4...），不是每 5 行。检查 `main.tex` 中 `\usepackage[review]{acl}` 是否正确启用了 `lineno`。如果行号不连续或有缺失，修复。确保行号在整篇论文中从第一行到最后一行连续显示。

如果 ACL style 文件的 `[review]` 选项本身就只显示每 5 行的编号，那就需要手动加 `\usepackage{lineno}` 和 `\linenumbers`（在 `\begin{document}` 之前），确保每行都有编号。

## 2. 第 5 页表和图的布局
当前第 5 页的 Table 和 Figure 都挤在同一侧。修改 float placement，让 Table 在左栏顶部、Figure 在右栏顶部（或反过来），视觉上一个在左一个在右。

具体看 `experiments.tex` 中 Table 2 (`tab:scaling`, line 70) 和 Figure 2 (line 95) 的位置。可以尝试：
- 调整它们在源码中的前后顺序
- 使用 `[t]` vs `[b]` 控制位置
- 或者把 Figure 2 提前到 Table 2 之前，让 LaTeX 自然分配到不同栏

**完成后**：编译，截图第 5 页验证布局，commit。

62	All three formulations achieve comparable accuracy on Qwen3-4B: \cls{} 88.43\% ($\pm$0.78), \cwm{} 87.19\% ($\pm$0.37), \traj{} 86.30\% ($\pm$0.97). DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B shows: \cls{} 86.85\%, \cwm{} 86.83\%, \traj{} 84.16\%. The \cls{}--\cwm{} gap (0.02--1.24\pp{}) is within seed noise in both families. However, the \cls{}--\traj{} per-test gap is 2.13\pp{} on Qwen3-4B and 2.69\pp{} on DeepSeek-Coder; the latter clearly exceeds the $\pm$2\pp{} equivalence margin (\S\ref{sec:equivalence}). \cls{} and \cwm{} are robustly equivalent; \traj{} parity is model-contingent. Zero-shot baselines (Tables~\ref{tab:main_results} and~\ref{tab:repo_level}) establish that repo-level verification requires fine-tuning (0-shot$\to$fine-tuned gains of 35--36\pp{}) and that formulation choice matters only at repo level (\cls{} outperforms \cwm{} by 18--26\pp{}).

_source line 6140 · 2026-05-25T07:58:24.159442+00:00_

1	\begin{abstract}
2	Execution-free verification promises to replace costly test execution in Large Language Model (LLM)-based program repair, yet practitioners must choose among three fundamentally different formulations with no controlled comparison to guide the decision: per-test classification (\cls{}), execution output generation (\cwm{}), and trajectory-level scoring (\traj{}). We present the first systematic comparison, controlling for model capacity, training data, and evaluation protocol across two code LLM families (Qwen3, DeepSeek-Coder), two complexity levels, and multiple random seeds under ${\leq}$8B Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) fine-tuning. Our central finding is that \emph{code complexity moderates the classification--generation tradeoff}. When outputs are short (function-level verification), all three formulations achieve statistically equivalent accuracy. When outputs grow longer and more structured (repository-level verification), this equivalence breaks down: generation-based verification suffers format collapse, falling well below classification. Preliminary results at 14B suggest this divergence narrows with scale, pointing to a capacity-dependent rather than formulation-inherent limitation. A failure mode taxonomy and error complementarity analysis provide actionable guidance for formulation selection and motivate complexity-aware routing as a key open direction.
3	\end{abstract}
4

_source line 6142 · 2026-05-25T07:58:24.612252+00:00_

1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	We presented the first systematic comparison of three execution-free verification formulations (\cls{}, \cwm{}, \traj{}) under ${\leq}$8B LoRA fine-tuning. At function level, \cls{} and \cwm{} are statistically equivalent (TOST $p{=}0.007$, 5 seeds); \traj{} equivalence is inconclusive after Holm correction. At repository level, divergence reaches 18--26\pp{} ($z{=}-23.7$) via three failure modes, consistent across families (\S\ref{sec:threats}). 14B results (3 seeds, single family) narrow all gaps to 1.07\pp{}; whether the repo-level divergence is formulation-inherent or capacity-contingent remains open. Scaling to 32B+~\cite{meta2025cwm} and complexity-aware routing are key open questions.
5	
6	\section*{Limitations}
7	
8	Primary findings use ${\leq}$8B LoRA fine-tuning with multi-seed replication; 14B results use a single model family (Qwen3) with 3 seeds; cross-family replication and evaluation at 32B+ are needed. The repo-level gap may diminish at 32B+ scale or with execution-trace pretraining~\cite{meta2025cwm}. All evaluation uses Python-only benchmarks; other languages may exhibit different format compliance rates. Training data uses synthetic mutation operators~\cite{andrews2005mutation,just2014mutants}; the synthetic-to-real transfer gap remains untested (Appendix~\ref{app:limitations}). Format-specific interventions (constrained decoding~\cite{poesia2022synchromesh,dong2024xgrammar,willard2023outlines} or output-format pretraining) may mitigate \cwm{}'s format collapse but were not evaluated.

502	Table~\ref{tab:holm} applies Holm-Bonferroni correction~\cite{holm1979} to the five primary statistical comparisons across this study ($k{=}5$, family-wise $\alpha{=}0.05$).
503	
504	\begin{table}[h]

524	The two core claims survive correction: the repo-level \cls{}--\cwm{} divergence (adjusted $p{=}0.015$) and function-level \cls{}--\cwm{} equivalence (adjusted $p{=}0.028$). The function-level \cls{}--\traj{} equivalence does not survive correction (adjusted $p{=}0.075$). Under problem-level clustered bootstrap, the raw TOST $p$ decreases to ${\sim}$0.007 (adjusted $p{\approx}0.021$), which would confirm equivalence; we report the more conservative seed-level result ($p{=}0.025$) as primary, since training seeds are the true unit of replication.

101	{\footnotesize \textsuperscript{$\ast$}Both models achieve 45.83\% by correctly classifying all 22 unresolved variants and zero resolved variants (22/48 = 45.83\%), equivalent to an always-predict-unresolved baseline. The 100\% overlap in variant-level predictions confirms complete degradation at task-relevant granularity.}
102	\end{table}
103	
104	Both models converge to exactly 45.83\% variant-level accuracy under 3-shot prompting, below majority baseline (54.17\%). Variant-level analysis reveals this is not a coincidence: both models correctly classify all 22 unresolved variants and zero resolved variants, equivalent to an always-predict-unresolved baseline with 100\% prediction overlap. Per-test accuracy diverges sharply (Qwen3-8B 71.61\% vs.\ DeepSeek 46.28\%), but this divergence is entirely superficial: Qwen3-8B predicts pass on nearly all tests, inflating per-test accuracy on a pass-heavy dataset (72.96\% pass rate) without genuine discriminative ability. Per-test metrics can thus severely misrepresent baseline capability; variant-level evaluation is necessary to detect complete degradation. Neither 0-shot nor few-shot prompting approaches fine-tuned performance (82--84\% per-test); fine-tuning is a necessary condition for repo-level verification.
105

593	\label{app:limitations}
594	The same-size 4B comparison uses a single seed; \cls{} 4B seed 123 (val-best ckpt-800) achieves only 75.33\% test accuracy (+2.4\pp{} above majority baseline); val-best selection at an early checkpoint (step 800) amplifies the val-test distribution shift observed across all configurations. The \cwm{} 8B comparison uses 3 seeds per model. The validation and test sets have zero problem overlap but differ in mutation type composition (Table~\ref{tab:mutation_distribution}): \texttt{operator\_swap} shifts from 19.1\% (val) to 34.3\% (test), the largest single-type distribution mismatch (+15.2\pp{}). All repo-level results use val-selected checkpoints, and checkpoint selection variance could contribute to observed differences. Our training data is synthetically constructed from mutation operators rather than real developer patches. While mutation testing literature establishes that mutants are reasonable proxies for real faults in test effectiveness studies~\cite{andrews2005mutation,just2014mutants}, these findings concern mutation \emph{adequacy} (whether tests that kill mutants also detect real faults), not whether verifiers trained on mutants transfer to real patches. However, the coupling effect underlying these results supports \emph{relative ranking} validity: a domain shift from synthetic mutations to real bugs may affect absolute accuracy levels, but our core finding (that formulation choice matters at repo level but not function level) concerns relative performance ordering, which is expected to be preserved under distributional shifts that affect all formulations equally. The synthetic-to-real transfer gap for absolute accuracy remains untested. The \traj{} training configuration differs from \cls{}/\cwm{}: smaller training set (${\sim}$400 vs.\ ${\sim}$70K instances), different learning rate ($2{\times}10^{-4}$ vs.\ $5{\times}10^{-5}$), and variant-level evaluation ($n{=}48$ vs.\ per-test $n{=}9{,}784$). - 每张表/图的数据来源：从服务器上的 eval 输出文件 / results 文件中找到原始数字，与表中数字逐一对账
- 表头、列名、单位、有效位数是否正确
- 图的坐标轴标签、图例、caption 与图内容是否一致
- 公式符号定义是否在首次出现时给出，前后是否一致

重点检查：
- Table 1 (main_results): 所有 accuracy 数字
- Table 2 (scaling): 4B/8B/14B 数字
- Table 3 (repo_level): repo-level 数字
- Table 4 (taxonomy): failure mode 分类数字
- Figure 2: 折线图数据点是否与 Table 2 一致
- Figure 3: oracle-stratified 数字来源
- 附录图表数字

## 2. 跨章节数字一致性
同一个指标在以下 6 处必须完全一致（包括精度）：
- Abstract 中的关键数字
- Introduction contributions 中的数字
- Method 中的超参描述
- 主表的数字
- Analysis (§4.6) 中引用的数字
- Conclusion 中复述的数字

具体核查：
- TOST p-value 在 abstract/intro/experiments/conclusion 中是否一致
- 18-26pp gap 在各处是否一致
- Function-level accuracy 数字（88.43%, 87.19% 等）
- 14B convergence 数字
- Majority baseline (51.75% func, 72.96% repo)
- Oracle 数字 (90.63%, 95.83%)

## 3. 与实验真值对账
- 连接服务器，找到实际 eval 输出文件，与 Table 1/2/3 的数字逐一比对
- 确认 reporting protocol：mean ± std 对应哪些 seeds
- 搜索所有 .tex 文件中的占位符：XX.X、TBD、---、\placeholder、\tbd

## 4. 输出格式
完成后汇报：
(a) 改了哪几张表/图的数字
(b) 一致性问题修在了哪几个章节
(c) 还有哪些数字是占位待补
(d) 数据来源无法验证的项（标注原因）

如果发现不一致**立即修正**并 commit。

1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	We systematically compare \cls{}, \cwm{}, and \traj{} across two representative code LLM families, two complexity levels, and multiple seeds to characterize the formulation landscape for neural code verification.
5	
6	\subsection{Experimental Setup}
7	\label{sec:setup}
8	
9	\paragraph{Datasets.}
10	\emph{Function-level}: mutated variants from HumanEval~\cite{chen2021humaneval} and MBPP~\cite{austin2021mbpp} (five mutation types), labels verified by execution. 19,076 samples (train 15,290 / val 1,896 / test 1,890; pass:fail $\approx$ 52:48, majority baseline 51.75\%).
11	\emph{Repo-level}: per-test instances from SWE-bench Verified~\cite{chowdhury2024swebenchverified} mutants. ${\sim}$70K training samples; val ($n{=}9{,}335$) and test ($n{=}9{,}784$) have zero problem overlap (majority baseline 72.96\%). Val-based checkpoint selection follows standard practice~\cite{jimenez2024swebench}; runs with NaN validation loss (due to truncated targets exceeding 2,048-token limits) default to the final epoch checkpoint (Appendix~\ref{app:hyperparams}). Qwen3 \cwm{} runs show early overfitting (best at ${\sim}$6\% of training); checkpoint details in Appendix~\ref{app:hyperparams}.
12	
13	\paragraph{Models and training.}
14	We fine-tune Qwen3-4B and Qwen3-8B~\cite{qwen2025qwen3} with Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA)~\cite{hu2022lora} (rank 16, 3 epochs) across five seeds. Seeds 42/123/456 use bfloat16; seeds 789/0 use NormalFloat 4-bit (NF4) quantization~\cite{dettmers2024qlora} to test robustness to precision reduction (function-level accuracy remains within seed noise across both precisions; see footnote in \S\ref{sec:equivalence}). As a cross-model consistency check, we additionally fine-tune DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B~\cite{guo2024deepseekcoder} with identical LoRA configuration (2 seeds each for \cls{} and \cwm{}). Our \cwm{} baseline is a LoRA fine-tuned variant for matched comparison, not a reproduction of the 32B Code World Model

_source line 6228 · 2026-05-25T08:31:25.551895+00:00_

1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	We presented the first systematic comparison of three execution-free verification formulations (\cls{}, \cwm{}, \traj{}) under ${\leq}$8B LoRA fine-tuning. At function level, \cls{} and \cwm{} are statistically equivalent (TOST $p{=}0.007$, 5 seeds); \traj{} equivalence is inconclusive after Holm correction. At repository level, divergence reaches 18--26\pp{} via three failure modes; the formulation$\times$complexity interaction is highly significant (interaction $z{=}-23.7$, $p{<}0.001$), consistent across families (\S\ref{sec:threats}). 14B results (3 seeds, single family) narrow all gaps to 1.07\pp{}; whether the repo-level divergence is formulation-inherent or capacity-contingent remains open. Scaling to 32B+~\cite{meta2025cwm} and complexity-aware routing are key open questions.
5	
6	\section*{Limitations}
7	
8	Primary findings use ${\leq}$8B LoRA fine-tuning with multi-seed replication; 14B results use a single model family (Qwen3) with 3 seeds; cross-family replication and evaluation at 32B+ are needed. The repo-level gap may diminish at 32B+ scale or with execution-trace pretraining~\cite{meta2025cwm}. All evaluation uses Python-only benchmarks; other languages may exhibit different format compliance rates. Training data uses synthetic mutation operators~\cite{andrews2005mutation,just2014mutants}; the synthetic-to-real transfer gap remains untested (Appendix~\ref{app:limitations}). Format-specific interventions (constrained decoding~\cite{poesia2022synchromesh,dong2024xgrammar,willard2023outlines} or output-format pretraining) may mitigate \cwm{}'s format collapse but were not evaluated.

_source line 6230 · 2026-05-25T08:31:25.753788+00:00_

1	\begin{abstract}
2	Execution-free verification promises to replace costly test execution in Large Language Model (LLM)-based program repair, yet practitioners must choose among three fundamentally different formulations with no controlled comparison to guide the decision: per-test classification (\cls{}), execution output generation (\cwm{}), and trajectory-level scoring (\traj{}). We present the first systematic comparison, controlling for model capacity, training data, and evaluation protocol across two code LLM families (Qwen3, DeepSeek-Coder), two complexity levels, and multiple random seeds under ${\leq}$8B Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) fine-tuning. Our central finding is that \emph{code complexity moderates the classification--generation tradeoff}. When outputs are short (function-level verification), all three formulations achieve statistically equivalent accuracy. When outputs grow longer and more structured (repository-level verification), this equivalence breaks down: generation-based verification suffers format collapse, falling well below classification. At 14B, function-level gaps narrow to within one percentage point, but whether the repo-level divergence is capacity-contingent remains open. A failure mode taxonomy and error complementarity analysis provide actionable guidance for formulation selection and motivate complexity-aware routing as a key open direction.
3	\end{abstract}
4

_source line 6232 · 2026-05-25T08:31:25.804750+00:00_

1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	LLM-based automated program repair systems increasingly rely on test-time compute scaling: generating $K$ candidate patches and selecting the best via a verifier~\cite{snell2024scaling}. Full execution verification is reliable but expensive (\$1--3 per candidate at repository scale\footnote{Based on per-instance costs reported by \citet{yang2024sweagent} and \citet{xia2024agentless}.}), driving demand for execution-free alternatives. Three distinct formulations have emerged for this task: \textbf{generation-based simulation}~\cite{meta2025cwm,maimon2026selfexecution} predicts execution output strings per test; \textbf{trajectory-level scoring}~\cite{shum2025swerm} predicts a binary patch verdict without per-test decomposition; and \textbf{per-test classification}~\cite{ni2023lever,chen2023codet} predicts a pass/fail token per test. Each makes different tradeoffs between diagnostic granularity, output complexity, and inference cost.
5	
6	Despite active development along each axis, no prior work provides a systematic comparison holding model capacity, training data, and compute constant. Practitioners choosing a formulation for their verification pipeline must rely on results from different models, different datasets, and different evaluation protocols. Whether one formulation is uniformly preferable, or whether the choice depends on properties of the code being verified, remains an open empirical question. This connects to a broader pattern in NLP: task formulation (classification vs.\ generation vs.\ span extraction) systematically affects performance~\cite{raffel2020t5}, but the conditions under which each wins remain task-dependent and empirically unresolved. Recent evidence that generation-based verification outperforms classification in mathematical reasoning~\cite{hosseini2025genrm} raises a further question: does this advantage transfer to code verification, where outputs are longer and format-constraine
…[truncated 1665 chars]

1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	Let $p$ denote a candidate patch and $T = \{t_1, \ldots, t_n\}$ its test suite, where each $t_i$ has label $y_i \in \{\text{\textsc{Pass}}, \text{\textsc{Fail}}\}$ from execution. Tests decompose into \emph{fail-to-pass} ($T_{\text{f2p}}$) and \emph{pass-to-pass} ($T_{\text{p2p}}$) subsets; a patch is correct iff $\forall\, t_i{:}\ y_i = \text{\textsc{Pass}}$. We compare three formulations for predicting correctness without execution.
5	
6	\subsection{Verification Formulations}
7	\label{sec:formulations}
8	
9	Let $c_p$ denote the code context (unified diff) of patch $p$.
10	
11	\paragraph{\cls{} (per-test classification).} $f(c_p, t_i) \rightarrow \hat{y}_i \in \{\text{\textsc{Pass}}, \text{\textsc{Fail}}\}$ predicts a single binary token per test (cross-entropy loss). Patch verdict: $\hat{y}_p = \bigwedge_{i} [\hat{y}_i = \text{\textsc{Pass}}]$. Output cost: one token per test.
12	
13	\paragraph{\cwm{} (output generation).}\footnote{We use ``code world model'' following \citet{meta2025cwm} to denote execution output prediction, distinct from world models in model-based reinforcement learning.} $g(c_p, t_i) \rightarrow \hat{o}_i \in \Sigma^*$ generates the execution output string (language modeling loss). Verdict by exact match: $\hat{y}_i = \text{\textsc{Pass}}$ iff $\hat{o}_i = o_i^*$.\footnote{Exact string matching is conservative, consistent with \citet{meta2025cwm}. Semantic equivalences are not counted.} When the generated output is unparseable (i.e., does not conform to the expected structured format), the prediction defaults to the majority class verdict. Output cost: $\sum_i |\hat{o}_i|$ tokens.
14	
15	\paragraph{\traj{} (trajectory-level).} $h(c_p, T) \rightarrow \hat{y}_p \in \{\text{\textsc{Resolved}}, \text{\textsc{Unresolved}}\}$ predicts a single patch verdict from all tests and provides no per-test signal. \traj{} serves as a granularity control: \cls{}--\traj{} differences isolate the value of per-test de
…[truncated 3514 chars]

66	Both rank-32 variants fall within the 2-seed variance of the rank-16 baseline (0.53\pp{} between seeds 42 and 456; Table~\ref{tab:rank_ablation}). The $\alpha{=}64$ variant (matching the baseline $\alpha/r{=}2$ ratio) rules out the confound that the $\alpha{=}32$ result might reflect reduced effective scaling rather than rank insensitivity. This is consistent with \cwm{}'s function-level performance being bounded by output-format constraints rather than adapter capacity.
67	
68	\paragraph{Majority voting.}
69	Majority voting ($K{=}3$) decreases accuracy from 87.51\% to 86.83\% ($-$0.68\pp{}): errors are systematic rather than stochastic, so sampling-based ensembles do not help.
70	
71	\paragraph{Sequence-length ablation details.}
72	Both formulations are trained with \texttt{max\_seq\_length}$=$2,048. At inference, \cwm{} reserves \texttt{max\_new\_tokens}$=$512, leaving ${\sim}$1,536 effective input tokens; \cls{} allocates nearly all context to input (5,120--8,192 tokens). A matched-context ablation (\cls{} 8B at 2,048 tokens) achieves 83.04\%, only 1.44\pp{} below the 8,192-token evaluation (84.48\%), though truncation introduces 136 unparseable outputs (1.39\% of test samples). 410	
411	The \cls{}--\traj{} efficiency comparison is more nuanced, because \cls{} makes one call per test while \traj{} makes one call per instance. Per-call, \cls{} is 20--138$\times$ faster (depending on input length: 47ms for short contexts vs.\ 2,632ms for \traj{}). However, the per-instance effective speedup depends on test count $|T|$: at function level ($|T| \approx 2.3$), \cls{} is ${\sim}$24$\times$ faster (108ms vs.\ 2,632ms per instance); at repo level ($|T| \approx 12.3$, ${\sim}$70ms/call with longer inputs), \cls{} is ${\sim}$3$\times$ faster (861ms vs.\ 2,631ms). A crossover exists at $|T| \approx 50$ tests, beyond which \traj{}'s single-call amortization dominates. This boundary condition is informative for deployment: \cls{} is strongly preferred for function-level and moderate repo-level test suites, while extremely large test suites ($>$50 tests per instance) favor trajectory-level efficiency despite its coarser diagnostic signal.
412	
413	\paragraph{(j) Re-repair pilot.}
414	We tested whether per-test diagnostic feedback improves downstream repair. A Qwen3-8B repair agent received either per-test diagnostic signals (which specific tests failed and why) or binary accept/reject feedback, evaluated on 44 SWE-bench Verified instances. The diagnostic condition achieved accuracy no higher than the binary baseline ($A_1 = 52.3\%$, $p = 0.42$, effect size $\approx 0$). Per-test diagnostic information does not automatically translate to repair improvements at this scale. The negative result may reflect insufficient agent capability to exploit fine-grained feedback, insufficient sample size (at $n = 44$, statistical power is ${\sim}$0.3 for a medium effect size), or a genuine limitation of the diagnostic-to-repair translation.
415	
416	\paragraph{(k) Relationship to chain-of-thought verification.}

[Director 指令] [Director 指令] **匿名仓库重整 + 论文链接位置规范化**

## Part 1: 匿名仓库自检与推送

### 1.1 密钥验证
- 读取 `_anonymous_repo.json` 中的 push_url
- 执行 `git push --dry-run &lt;push_url> master:main` 验证 token 有效
- 如失效，立即报告

### 1.2 仓库内容自检
在 push 之前，检查本地 repo：

**匿名性**：
- `git log --format='%an <%ae> | %cn <%ce>' | sort -u` 确认 commit author/committer 无真实姓名或邮箱
- 如果有非匿名 author，用 `git filter-branch` 或 `git rebase` 重写 commit history
- 搜索所有文件中的真实姓名、机构名（搜索常见模式：大学名、实验室名、人名）
- README.md / LICENSE / 代码注释 / requirements.txt 检查

**完整性**：
- README.md 写清楚：环境依赖、如何安装、入口脚本、数据获取方式
- 核心代码（训练/评测脚本）都在
- requirements.txt 或 environment.yml 能 reproduce

**干净度**：
- 删除 `.DS_Store`、`__pycache__/`、`*.pyc`、`.env`、`wandb/`、临时日志
- 确保 `.gitignore` 覆盖这些
- 删除未引用的死代码
- 确保 `_anonymous_repo.json` 在 `.gitignore` 中

**数据可获取**：
- README 标注数据来源和获取方式

### 1.3 推送
自检完毕后 force push：
```
git push --force &lt;push_url> master:main
```
（remote 是 main branch，本地是 master）

_source line 6292 · 2026-05-25T08:42:48.271226+00:00_

13	\paragraph{Models and training.}
14	We fine-tune Qwen3-4B and Qwen3-8B~\cite{qwen2025qwen3} with Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA)~\cite{hu2022lora} (rank 16, 3 epochs) across five seeds. Seeds 42/123/456 use bfloat16; seeds 789/0 use NormalFloat 4-bit (NF4) quantization~\cite{dettmers2024qlora} to test robustness to precision reduction (function-level accuracy remains within seed noise across both precisions; see footnote in \S\ref{sec:equivalence}). As a cross-model consistency check, we additionally fine-tune DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B~\cite{guo2024deepseekcoder} with identical LoRA configuration (2 seeds each for \cls{} and \cwm{}). Our \cwm{} baseline is a LoRA fine-tuned variant for matched comparison, not a reproduction of the 32B Code World Model~\cite{meta2025cwm}. \traj{} uses variant-level rather than per-test labels (8,052 vs.\ 15,290 training instances, but covering $2.24\times$ more unique code variants); the comparison is confounded by both label granularity and data composition (\S\ref{sec:repo}). Repo-level models use val-selected checkpoints where available; runs with NaN validation loss use the final epoch checkpoint (Appendix~\ref{app:hyperparams}). Zero-shot baselines use Qwen3-8B and Qwen3-32B (NF4) as LLM-as-judge. Full hyperparameter details appear in Appendix~\ref{app:hyperparams}; prompt templates in Appendix~\ref{app:prompts}.\footnote{Code and data: \url{https://github.com/anonymous-nlp-2026-2/exec-sim-repair-verifier}}

_source line 6296 · 2026-05-25T08:42:58.060033+00:00_

1	\begin{abstract}
2	Execution-free verification promises to replace costly test execution in Large Language Model (LLM)-based program repair, yet practitioners must choose among three fundamentally different formulations with no controlled comparison to guide the decision: per-test classification (\cls{}), execution output generation (\cwm{}), and trajectory-level scoring (\traj{}). We present the first systematic comparison, controlling for model capacity, training data, and evaluation protocol across two code LLM families (Qwen3, DeepSeek-Coder), two complexity levels, and multiple random seeds under ${\leq}$8B Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) fine-tuning. Our central finding is that \emph{code complexity moderates the classification--generation tradeoff}. When outputs are short (function-level verification), all three formulations achieve statistically equivalent accuracy. When outputs grow longer and more structured (repository-level verification), this equivalence breaks down: generation-based verification suffers format collapse, falling well below classification. At 14B, function-level gaps narrow to approximately one percentage point, but whether the repo-level divergence is capacity-contingent remains open. A failure mode taxonomy and error complementarity analysis provide actionable guidance for formulation selection and motivate complexity-aware routing as a key open direction.
3	\end{abstract}
4

==> train_unified.py <==
"""Unified LoRA fine-tuning for CWM (Code World Model) generation — supports multiple model families.

Supported model families:
  - Qwen3 (e.g. Qwen/Qwen3-4B, Qwen/Qwen3-8B)
  - DeepSeek-Coder (e.g. deepseek-ai/deepseek-coder-6.7b-instruct)

==> train_cwm_baseline.py <==
# train_cwm_baseline.py — Qwen3 LoRA fine-tuning for CWM (Code World Model) generation baseline
# Input: data_dir/ with {cwm_train,cwm_val}.jsonl (fields: code, test, target)
# Output: LoRA adapter in output_dir/best/
# Key difference from train_verifier.py (classification):
#   - Prompt asks for execution result instead of pass/fail

==> build_data.py <==
# Build (code, test) -> pass/fail training data from HumanEval + MBPP
# Mutations on canonical solutions generate fail examples; actual execution verifies labels.

import argparse
import ast

==> build_cwm_data.py <==
# build_cwm_data.py — Convert (code, test, label) data to CWM generation format
# Input: data/{train,val,test}.jsonl with fields: code, test, label, source, problem_id, mutation_type
# Output: data/{cwm_train,cwm_val,cwm_test}.jsonl with target = execution output string
# Key difference from classification: label 0/1 → target string with actual error messages
# For fail samples, re-executes code+test to capture stderr; pass samples get fixed string.
