{
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_16/projects/exec-sim-repair-verifier/README.md",
  "content": "# Execution-Free Repair Verification: Classification vs. Generation vs. Trajectory\n\nCode and data for the paper *\"Execution-Free Repair Verification: A Systematic Comparison of Classification, Generation, and Trajectory Formulations\"* (EMNLP 2026 submission).\n\n## Setup\n\n```bash\npip install -r requirements.txt\n```\n\nPyTorch with CUDA is required for training and inference. See [pytorch.org](https://pytorch.org/) for installation instructions matching your CUDA version.\n\n## Data\n\n### Function-level data (HumanEval + MBPP)\n\n```bash\n# Build classification data (code, test) -> pass/fail\npython build_data.py --output_dir data/\n\n# Convert to CWM generation format (code, test) -> execution output\npython build_cwm_data.py --input_dir data/ --output_dir data/\n```\n\n### Repository-level data (SWE-bench)\n\nRepository-level data requires the [SWE-bench](https://github.com/princeton-nlp/SWE-bench) package. See `build_data.py --help` for SWE-bench data options.\n\n## Training\n\nAll formulations (CLS, CWM, TRAJ) use LoRA fine-tuning via a unified training script:\n\n```bash\n# CLS (per-test classification)\npython train_unified.py \\\n  --model_name Qwen/Qwen3-4B \\\n  --data_dir data/ \\\n  --formulation cls \\\n  --output_dir checkpoints/cls_qwen3_4b/ \\\n  --seed 42\n\n# CWM (execution output generation)\npython train_unified.py \\\n  --model_name Qwen/Qwen3-4B \\\n  --data_dir data/ \\\n  --formulation cwm \\\n  --output_dir checkpoints/cwm_qwen3_4b/ \\\n  --seed 42\n\n# TRAJ (trajectory-level scoring)\npython train_unified.py \\\n  --model_name Qwen/Qwen3-4B \\\n  --data_dir data/ \\\n  --formulation traj \\\n  --output_dir checkpoints/traj_qwen3_4b/ \\\n  --seed 42\n```\n\nSupported models: `Qwen/Qwen3-4B`, `Qwen/Qwen3-8B`, `Qwen/Qwen3-14B`, `deepseek-ai/deepseek-coder-6.7b-instruct`.\n\n## Evaluation\n\n```bash\n# CLS / TRAJ evaluation\npython eval_majority_voting.py \\\n  --checkpoint checkpoints/cls_qwen3_4b/best/ \\\n  --data_dir data/ \\\n  --output results/cls_qwen3_4b.json\n\n# CWM evaluation\npython eval_cwm_baseline.py \\\n  --checkpoint checkpoints/cwm_qwen3_4b/best/ \\\n  --data_dir data/ \\\n  --output results/cwm_qwen3_4b.json\n\n# 14B zero-shot / few-shot baselines\npython eval_14b_baseline.py \\\n  --model_name Qwen/Qwen3-14B \\\n  --data_dir data/ \\\n  --output results/14b_baseline.json\n```\n\n## Analysis\n\n```bash\n# TOST equivalence testing and sensitivity analysis\npython tost_sensitivity.py\n\n# ICC computation for oracle accuracy framework\npython compute_deepseek_func_icc.py\n\n# Supplementary statistics (bootstrap CIs, interaction tests)\npython compute_supplementary_stats.py\n\n# CWM format compliance and error analysis\npython analyze_cwm_eval.py --results_dir results/\npython cwm_forced_parse.py --results_dir results/\npython cwm_parseable_bias.py --results_dir results/\n\n# Routing analysis (error complementarity)\npython routing_analysis.py --results_dir results/\n```\n\n## Re-repair (downstream application)\n\n```bash\n# Generate re-repair candidates using CLS verifier feedback\npython cls_inference_for_rerepair.py \\\n  --checkpoint checkpoints/cls_qwen3_8b/best/ \\\n  --data_dir data/\n\n# Run re-repair pipeline\npython re_repair_v2.py --data_dir data/ --output_dir results/rerepair/\n\n# Evaluate re-repair results\npython re_repair_eval.py --results_dir results/rerepair/\n```\n\n## LoRA Adapter Merging\n\n```bash\npython merge_lora.py --base_model Qwen/Qwen3-4B --adapter checkpoints/cls_qwen3_4b/best/ --output merged_model/\n```\n\n## Repository Structure\n\n```\nbuild_data.py              # Function-level data generation (HumanEval + MBPP mutations)\nbuild_cwm_data.py          # Convert classification data to CWM generation format\ntrain_unified.py           # Unified LoRA fine-tuning for CLS/CWM/TRAJ\ntrain_cwm_baseline.py      # CWM-specific training (legacy, use train_unified.py)\neval_cwm_baseline.py       # CWM generation evaluation\neval_14b_baseline.py       # 14B zero/few-shot baselines\neval_majority_voting.py    # Per-test majority voting evaluation\neval_cwm_constrained.py    # CWM with constrained decoding\ntost_sensitivity.py        # TOST equivalence margin sensitivity\ncompute_deepseek_func_icc.py  # Intra-class correlation for oracle framework\ncompute_supplementary_stats.py # Bootstrap CIs, interaction z-tests\nanalyze_cwm_eval.py        # CWM error analysis\ncwm_forced_parse.py        # Forced parsing of CWM outputs\ncwm_parseable_bias.py      # Parseable output bias analysis\nrouting_analysis.py        # Error complementarity / routing analysis\ncls_inference_for_rerepair.py # CLS inference for re-repair pipeline\nre_repair_v2.py            # Re-repair pipeline\nre_repair_eval.py          # Re-repair evaluation\nmerge_lora.py              # Merge LoRA adapter into base model\ndocs/paper/                # LaTeX source for the paper\n```\n\n## License\n\nThis repository is released for research purposes under the MIT License.\n"
}

_source line 6372 · 2026-05-25T09:06:08.139510+00:00_

[Director 指令] [Director 指令] **附录引用完整性检查（用户要求）**

## 任务
检查论文附录每个 section/subsection/table/figure 是否在正文中有合理的引用指向。

## 步骤

### 1. 盘点附录
读取 `appendix.tex`，列出所有 section/subsection/table/figure/algorithm，记录每个的 `\label{}`。

### 2. 逐项验证引用
对每个附录 label，在所有正文 .tex 文件（abstract/introduction/related_work/method/experiments/conclusion）中搜索 `\ref{该label}` 或文字性引用（"Appendix A" 等）。

判定标准：
- ✅ 有引用，且在内容相关的章节（不是只在 conclusion/limitations 末尾提一句）
- ⚠️ 有引用但质量差（只说 "see Appendix X" 没说明为什么要去看）
- ❌ 完全无引用（孤儿附录）

### 3. 修复
对 ❌ 和 ⚠️ 的项：
- 在最相关的正文章节加一句自然引用，说明读者去那里能找到什么、解决什么疑问
- 例如："Full hyperparameter settings including learning rate schedule are reported in Appendix~\ref{app:hyperparams}."
- 不要硬塞 "See Appendix X."
- 如果某个附录 section 确实与正文无关联且不支撑任何 claim，标记为可删除（但不要自行删除，先报告）

### 4. 汇报格式
```
(a) 附录共 N 个 section，M 个 table，K 个 figure
(b) 孤儿附录：列出原本无引用的，现在加在哪个章节
(c) 被判定无必要的：列出（如有）
```

**修复后 commit，确认正文仍 ≤ 8 页。**

=== app:oracle ===
method.tex:33:For a buggy patch with $k = |T_{\text{f2p}}|$ fail-to-pass tests, a per-test verifier with recall $r$ detects the bug if it identifies at least one failing test: $P_{\text{detect}}(k) = 1 - (1 - r)^{k_{\text{eff}}}$, where $k_{\text{eff}} = k / (1 + (k-1)\rho)$ accounts for within-patch correlation via ICC $\rho$~\citep{shrout1979icc}. The per-test advantage over a trajectory-level baseline ($a_h$) is $\Delta(k) = P_{\text{detect}}(k) - a_h$, with population expectation $\mathbb{E}[\Delta] = \sum_{k} P(|T_{\text{f2p}}| = k) \cdot \Delta(k)$. This predicts that per-test advantage concentrates in multi-F2P-test instances and vanishes at $k{=}1$, validated in \S\ref{sec:experiments} and Appendix~\ref{app:oracle}.
experiments.tex:191:\paragraph{Error complementarity.} \cls{} and \cwm{} exhibit partially complementary errors, suggesting potential complementarity for routing. An oracle combination achieves 90.63\% (+2.80\pp{}; 95\% CI [89.3\%, 91.9\%]; single-seed) at function level and 95.83\% (+14.58\pp{}; $n{=}48$) at repo level. Simple routing heuristics capture $<$0.4\pp{} of the oracle gap; the complementarity is instance-specific, suggesting that simple heuristics are insufficient; whether a learned router can capture this advantage remains open (Appendix~\ref{app:oracle}).
=== app:ablations ===
experiments.tex:180:\paragraph{Complexity as moderator.} The \cls{}--\cwm{} gap widens from 1--3\pp{} (function) to 18--26\pp{} (repo) under our protocol; a matched-context ablation confirms input disparity explains $<$3\% of this gap (Appendix~\ref{app:ablations}). While the directional advantage of lower-complexity output formulations may be anticipated from \S\ref{sec:failure-modes}, the magnitude of this gap, the function-level equivalence, and the specific failure mechanisms (Table~\ref{tab:taxonomy}) were not predictable a priori.

experiments.tex:112:We test pairwise equivalence on 523 variant-level instances across five seeds.\footnote{Seeds 789/0 use NF4 inference; the remaining three use bfloat16. Restricting to bfloat16 only: \cls{}--\cwm{} remains significant ($p{<}0.05$); \cls{}--\traj{} yields $p{=}0.128$.} Our Smallest Effect Size of Interest (SESOI) threshold of $\pm$2\pp{}~\cite{lakens2017,wellek2010} is domain-motivated but not pre-registered: at 85--88\% accuracy, 2\pp{} corresponds to ${\sim}$2--3 problems out of 114, below practitioner-relevant differences, and is consistent with between-seed variation (0.37--0.97\pp{} std). Within-problem predictions are correlated (ICC $= 0.191$, design effect [DEFF] $= 3.97$~\cite{moulton1990}); confidence intervals use clustered bootstrap (\S\ref{sec:setup}). Sensitivity analysis shows \cls{}--\cwm{} equivalence is robust across $\pm$1.5--3\pp{} margins (minimum $\pm$1.52\pp{}); \cls{}--\traj{} equivalence is margin-sensitive ($\pm$1.5\pp{}: $p{=}0.068$; Table~\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity}).
experiments.tex:114:\paragraph{\cls{}--\cwm{} equivalence.} The pooled gap is 1.24\pp{} (88.43\%--87.19\%); TOST confirms equivalence at $p = 0.007$ ($\pm$2\pp{} margin), surviving Holm-Bonferroni correction ($k{=}5$, adjusted $p{=}0.028$; Table~\ref{tab:holm}). Equivalence holds at margins as tight as $\pm$1.52\pp{} (Table~\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity}). On DeepSeek-Coder, the \cls{}--\cwm{} gap of 0.02\pp{} is consistent with equivalence but underpowered (2 seeds).

189	\paragraph{Why does generation degrade at repo level?} Output complexity increases nonlinearly: function-level outputs average ${\sim}$5--15 tokens; repo-level outputs average ${\sim}$477 tokens, leading to format collapse under our ${\leq}$8B LoRA protocol (53--74\% unparseable; representative error examples in Appendix~\ref{app:qualitative}). Even parseable outputs achieve only 38--43\%, below majority; a model accidentally trained on CLS-format data achieves 86.05\% with 0\% unparseable, confirming the degradation stems from output format complexity, not task difficulty (Appendix~\ref{app:extended_analysis}(c)). Four targeted interventions (doubling LoRA rank, majority voting [$k{=}5$], relaxed matching, and scaling to 8B) all fail to close this gap (Appendix~\ref{app:extended_analysis}(g)); checkpoint selection explains $<$1\pp{} (Appendix~\ref{app:cwm_convergence}). \cls{} achieves 217$\times$ higher throughput (1 vs.\ avg 477 output tokens; Appendix~\ref{app:extended_analysis}(i)).
190

[Director 指令] [Director 指令] **审计 Critical + Warnings 修复**

## Critical: Abstract "all three equivalent" overclaim

`abstract.tex:2` 说 "all three formulations achieve statistically equivalent accuracy"，但 `conclusion.tex:4` 写 "TRAJ equivalence is inconclusive after Holm correction"（adj p=0.075）。`experiments.tex:62` 也用了 "CLS and CWM are robustly equivalent; TRAJ parity is model-contingent"。

**修复**：Abstract 中 "all three formulations achieve statistically equivalent accuracy" 改为更精确的表述，例如：
"classification and generation achieve statistically equivalent accuracy; trajectory-level parity is model-dependent"
确保与 Conclusion 和 Experiments 的措辞一致。

## Warning 修复（4 项）

_source line 6390 · 2026-05-25T09:20:10.151273+00:00_

1	\begin{abstract}
2	Execution-free verification promises to replace costly test execution in Large Language Model (LLM)-based program repair, yet practitioners must choose among three fundamentally different formulations with no controlled comparison to guide the decision: per-test classification (\cls{}), execution output generation (\cwm{}), and trajectory-level scoring (\traj{}). We present the first systematic comparison, controlling for model capacity, training data, and evaluation protocol across two code LLM families (Qwen3, DeepSeek-Coder), two complexity levels, and multiple random seeds under ${\leq}$8B Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) fine-tuning. Our central finding is that \emph{code complexity moderates the classification--generation tradeoff}. When outputs are short (function-level verification), all three formulations achieve statistically equivalent accuracy. When outputs grow longer and more structured (repository-level verification), this equivalence breaks down: generation-based verification suffers format collapse, falling well below classification. At 14B, function-level gaps narrow to approximately one percentage point, but whether the repo-level divergence is capacity-contingent remains open. A failure mode taxonomy and error complementarity analysis provide actionable guidance for formulation selection and motivate complexity-aware routing as a key open direction. We release our code and data at\footnote{\url{https://github.com/anonymous-nlp-2026-2/exec-sim-repair-verifier}}.
3	\end{abstract}
4

1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	LLM-based automated program repair systems increasingly rely on test-time compute scaling: generating $K$ candidate patches and selecting the best via a verifier~\cite{snell2024scaling}. Full execution verification is reliable but expensive (\$1--3 per candidate at repository scale\footnote{Based on per-instance costs reported by \citet{yang2024sweagent} and \citet{xia2024agentless}.}), driving demand for execution-free alternatives. Three distinct formulations have emerged for this task: \textbf{generation-based simulation}~\cite{meta2025cwm,maimon2026selfexecution} predicts execution output strings per test; \textbf{trajectory-level scoring}~\cite{shum2025swerm} predicts a binary patch verdict without per-test decomposition; and \textbf{per-test classification}~\cite{ni2023lever,chen2023codet} predicts a pass/fail token per test. Each makes different tradeoffs between diagnostic granularity, output complexity, and inference cost.
5	
6	Despite active development along each axis, no prior work provides a systematic comparison holding model capacity, training data, and compute constant. Practitioners choosing a formulation for their verification pipeline must rely on results from different models, different datasets, and different evaluation protocols. Whether one formulation is uniformly preferable, or whether the choice depends on properties of the code being verified, remains an open empirical question. This connects to a broader pattern in NLP: task formulation (classification vs.\ generation vs.\ span extraction) systematically affects performance~\cite{raffel2020t5}, but the conditions under which each wins remain task-dependent and empirically unresolved. Recent evidence that generation-based verification outperforms classification in mathematical reasoning~\cite{hosseini2025genrm} raises a further question: does this advantage transfer to code verification, where outputs are longer and format-constraine
…[truncated 1665 chars]

1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	Let $p$ denote a candidate patch and $T = \{t_1, \ldots, t_n\}$ its test suite, where each $t_i$ has label $y_i \in \{\text{\textsc{Pass}}, \text{\textsc{Fail}}\}$ from execution. Tests decompose into \emph{fail-to-pass} ($T_{\text{f2p}}$) and \emph{pass-to-pass} ($T_{\text{p2p}}$) subsets; a patch is correct iff $\forall\, t_i{:}\ y_i = \text{\textsc{Pass}}$. We compare three formulations for predicting correctness without execution.
5	
6	\subsection{Verification Formulations}
7	\label{sec:formulations}
8	
9	Let $c_p$ denote the code context (unified diff) of patch $p$.
10	
11	\paragraph{\cls{} (per-test classification).} $f(c_p, t_i) \rightarrow \hat{y}_i \in \{\text{\textsc{Pass}}, \text{\textsc{Fail}}\}$ predicts a single binary token per test (cross-entropy loss). Patch verdict: $\hat{y}_p = \bigwedge_{i} [\hat{y}_i = \text{\textsc{Pass}}]$. Output cost: one token per test.
12	
13	\paragraph{\cwm{} (output generation).}\footnote{We use ``code world model'' following \citet{meta2025cwm} to denote execution output prediction, distinct from world models in model-based reinforcement learning.} $g(c_p, t_i) \rightarrow \hat{o}_i \in \Sigma^*$ generates the execution output string (language modeling loss). Verdict by exact match: $\hat{y}_i = \text{\textsc{Pass}}$ iff $\hat{o}_i = o_i^*$.\footnote{Exact string matching is conservative, consistent with \citet{meta2025cwm}. Semantic equivalences are not counted.} When the generated output is unparseable (i.e., does not conform to the expected structured format), the prediction defaults to the majority class verdict. Output cost: $\sum_i |\hat{o}_i|$ tokens.
14	
15	\paragraph{\traj{} (trajectory-level).} $h(c_p, T) \rightarrow \hat{y}_p \in \{\text{\textsc{Resolved}}, \text{\textsc{Unresolved}}\}$ predicts a single patch verdict from all tests and provides no per-test signal. \traj{} serves as a granularity control: \cls{}--\traj{} differences isolate the value of per-test de
…[truncated 3514 chars]

151	Few-shot prompting (3-shot ICL) collapses to an always-predict-unresolved baseline (variant accuracy 45.83\%, below majority 54.17\%; Appendix~\ref{app:few_shot}), establishing that execution-free verification requires task-specific fine-tuning regardless of formulation. The fine-tuning requirement (35--36\pp{} gains, Table~\ref{tab:repo_level}) is the first layer of our three-layer structure.

Limited seed coverage and multiple confounds (\S\ref{sec:threats}) limit causal attribution.

185	\label{sec:analysis}
186	
187	\paragraph{Aggregation penalty.} The aggregation penalty ranges from 1.36\pp{} to 19\pp{}, negligible at function level ($\bar{T} \approx 2.3$) but substantial at repo level ($\bar{T} \gg 10$; Appendix~\ref{app:extended_analysis}(a)).
188	
189	\paragraph{Why does generation degrade at repo level?} Output complexity increases nonlinearly: function-level outputs average ${\sim}$5--15 tokens; repo-level outputs average ${\sim}$477 tokens, leading to format collapse under our ${\leq}$8B LoRA protocol (53--74\% unparseable; representative error examples in Appendix~\ref{app:qualitative}). Even parseable outputs achieve only 38--43\%, below majority; a model accidentally trained on CLS-format data achieves 86.05\% with 0\% unparseable, confirming the degradation stems from output format complexity, not task difficulty (Appendix~\ref{app:extended_analysis}(c)). Four targeted interventions (doubling LoRA rank, majority voting [$k{=}5$], relaxed matching, and scaling to 8B) all fail to close this gap (Appendix~\ref{app:extended_analysis}(g)); checkpoint selection explains $<$1\pp{} (Appendix~\ref{app:cwm_convergence}). \cls{} achieves 217$\times$ higher throughput (1 vs.\ avg 477 output tokens; Appendix~\ref{app:extended_analysis}(i)).
190	
191	\paragraph{Error complementarity.} \cls{} and \cwm{} exhibit partially complementary errors, suggesting potential complementarity for routing. An oracle combination achieves 90.63\% (+2.80\pp{}; 95\% CI [89.3\%, 91.9\%]; single-seed) at function level and 95.83\% (+14.58\pp{}; $n{=}48$) at repo level. Simple routing heuristics capture $<$0.4\pp{} of the oracle gap; the complementarity is instance-specific, suggesting that simple heuristics are insufficient; whether a learned router can capture this advantage remains open (Appendix~\ref{app:oracle}).
192

_source line 6418 · 2026-05-25T09:21:02.909829+00:00_

1	\begin{abstract}
2	Execution-free verification promises to replace costly test execution in Large Language Model (LLM)-based program repair, yet practitioners must choose among three fundamentally different formulations with no controlled comparison to guide the decision: per-test classification (\cls{}), execution output generation (\cwm{}), and trajectory-level scoring (\traj{}). We present the first systematic comparison, controlling for model capacity, training data, and evaluation protocol across two code LLM families (Qwen3, DeepSeek-Coder), two complexity levels, and multiple random seeds under ${\leq}$8B Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) fine-tuning. Our central finding is that \emph{code complexity moderates the classification--generation tradeoff}. When outputs are short (function-level verification), classification and generation achieve statistically equivalent accuracy; trajectory-level parity is model-dependent. When outputs grow longer and more structured (repository-level verification), this equivalence breaks down: generation-based verification suffers format collapse, falling well below classification. At 14B, function-level gaps narrow to approximately one percentage point, but whether the repo-level divergence is capacity-contingent remains open. A failure mode taxonomy and error complementarity analysis provide actionable guidance for formulation selection and motivate complexity-aware routing as a key open direction. We release our code and data at\footnote{\url{https://github.com/anonymous-nlp-2026-2/exec-sim-repair-verifier}}.
3	\end{abstract}
4

62	All three formulations achieve comparable accuracy on Qwen3-4B: \cls{} 88.43\% ($\pm$0.78), \cwm{} 87.19\% ($\pm$0.37), \traj{} 86.30\% ($\pm$0.97). DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B shows: \cls{} 86.85\%, \cwm{} 86.83\%, \traj{} 84.16\%. The \cls{}--\cwm{} gap (0.02--1.24\pp{}) is within seed noise in both families. However, the \cls{}--\traj{} per-test gap is 2.13\pp{} on Qwen3-4B and 2.69\pp{} on DeepSeek-Coder; the latter clearly exceeds the $\pm$2\pp{} equivalence margin (\S\ref{sec:equivalence}). \cls{} and \cwm{} are robustly equivalent; \traj{} parity is model-contingent. Zero-shot baselines (Tables~\ref{tab:main_results} and~\ref{tab:repo_level}) establish that repo-level verification requires fine-tuning (0-shot$\to$fine-tuned gains of 35--36\pp{}) and that formulation choice matters only at repo level (\cls{} outperforms \cwm{} by 18--26\pp{}).

0	\section{Experiments}
1	\label{sec:experiments}
2	
3	We systematically compare \cls{}, \cwm{}, and \traj{} across two representative code LLM families, two complexity levels, and multiple seeds to characterize the formulation landscape for neural code verification.
4	
5	\subsection{Experimental Setup}
6	\label{sec:setup}
7	
8	\paragraph{Datasets.}
9	\emph{Function-level}: mutated variants from HumanEval~\cite{chen2021humaneval} and MBPP~\cite{austin2021mbpp} (five mutation types), labels verified by execution. 19,076 samples (train 15,290 / val 1,896 / test 1,890; pass:fail $\approx$ 52:48, majority baseline 51.75\%).
10	\emph{Repo-level}: per-test instances from SWE-bench Verified~\cite{chowdhury2024swebenchverified} mutants. ${\sim}$70K training samples; val ($n{=}9{,}335$) and test ($n{=}9{,}784$) have zero problem overlap (majority baseline 72.96\%). Val-based checkpoint selection follows standard practice~\cite{jimenez2024swebench}; runs with NaN validation loss (due to truncated targets exceeding 2,048-token limits) default to the final epoch checkpoint (Appendix~\ref{app:hyperparams}). Qwen3 \cwm{} runs show early overfitting (best at ${\sim}$6\% of training); checkpoint details in Appendix~\ref{app:hyperparams}.
11	
12	\paragraph{Models and training.}
13	We fine-tune Qwen3-4B and Qwen3-8B~\cite{qwen2025qwen3} with Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA)~\cite{hu2022lora} (rank 16, 3 epochs) across five seeds. Seeds 42/123/456 use bfloat16; seeds 789/0 use NormalFloat 4-bit (NF4) quantization~\cite{dettmers2024qlora} to test robustness to precision reduction (function-level accuracy remains within seed noise across both precisions; see footnote in \S\ref{sec:equivalence}). As a cross-model consistency check, we additionally fine-tune DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B~\cite{guo2024deepseekcoder} with identical LoRA configuration (2 seeds each for \cls{} and \cwm{}). Our \cwm{} baseline is a LoRA fine-tuned variant for matched comparison, not a reproduction of the 32B Code World Model~

177	
178	\paragraph{Default assignment artifact.} The apparent \cwm{} accuracy increase from 4B to 8B is an artifact of rising unparseable rate (53.7\%$\to$73.8\%), inflating accuracy via majority-class default while parseable-only accuracy decreases (42.88\%$\to$38.52\%); cross-seed evidence confirms the pattern (Appendix~\ref{app:parseable_bias}).
179	
180	\paragraph{Complexity as moderator.} The \cls{}--\cwm{} gap widens from 1--3\pp{} (function) to 18--26\pp{} (repo) under our protocol; a matched-context ablation confirms input disparity explains $<$3\% of this gap (Appendix~\ref{app:ablations}). While the directional advantage of lower-complexity output formulations may be anticipated from \S\ref{sec:failure-modes}, the magnitude of this gap, the function-level equivalence, and the specific failure mechanisms (Table~\ref{tab:taxonomy}) were not predictable a priori.
181	
182	\paragraph{Formulation$\times$complexity interaction.} A mixed-effects linear probability model (LPM) ($n{=}56{,}480$, 139 clusters, DeepSeek-Coder) confirms: \cwm{} effect at function level is negligible ($\hat{\beta}{=}-0.002$, $p{=}0.805$), but the formulation$\times$complexity interaction is highly significant ($z{=}-23.7$, $p{<}0.001$). A cross-model LPM confirms formulation dominates ($\hat{\beta}{=}-0.22$ vs.\ model $\hat{\beta}{=}+0.011$); model identity significance is estimator-dependent (LPM $p{=}0.002$; generalized estimating equations [GEE] $p{=}0.449$), consistent with limited clusters ($k{=}25 < 40$; Appendix~\ref{app:interaction}).
183

_source line 6444 · 2026-05-25T11:10:39.453216+00:00_

1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	We presented the first systematic comparison of three execution-free verification formulations (\cls{}, \cwm{}, \traj{}) under ${\leq}$8B LoRA fine-tuning. At function level, \cls{} and \cwm{} are statistically equivalent (TOST $p{=}0.007$, 5 seeds); \traj{} equivalence is inconclusive after Holm correction. At repository level, divergence reaches 18--26\pp{} via three failure modes; the formulation$\times$complexity interaction is highly significant (interaction $z{=}-23.7$, $p{<}0.001$), consistent across families (\S\ref{sec:threats}). 14B results (3 seeds, single family) narrow all gaps to 1.07\pp{}; whether the repo-level divergence is formulation-inherent or capacity-contingent remains open. Scaling to 32B+~\cite{meta2025cwm} and complexity-aware routing are key open questions.
5	
6	\section*{Limitations}
7	
8	Primary findings use ${\leq}$8B LoRA fine-tuning with multi-seed replication; 14B results use a single model family (Qwen3) with 3 seeds; cross-family replication and evaluation at 32B+ are needed. The repo-level gap may diminish at 32B+ scale or with execution-trace pretraining~\cite{meta2025cwm}. All evaluation uses Python-only benchmarks; other languages may exhibit different format compliance rates. Training data uses synthetic mutation operators~\cite{andrews2005mutation,just2014mutants}; the synthetic-to-real transfer gap remains untested (Appendix~\ref{app:limitations}). Format-specific interventions (constrained decoding~\cite{poesia2022synchromesh,dong2024xgrammar,willard2023outlines} or output-format pretraining) may mitigate \cwm{}'s format collapse but were not evaluated.

72	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/paper/figure2_v3.pdf}
73	\caption{Function-level accuracy scaling (4B--14B). CLS and CWM accuracy with shaded gap region; $y$-axis does not start at zero. The \cls{}--\cwm{} gap shows a non-monotonic pattern: 1.32\pp{} at 4B, widening to 2.62\pp{} at 8B, then narrowing to 0.43\pp{} at 14B (3 seeds).}
74	\label{fig:scaling_gap}
75	\end{figure}
76	
77	\cls{} and \traj{} both scale positively from 4B to 8B (+1.27\pp{} and +2.13\pp{} respectively), while \cwm{} remains flat ($-$0.03\pp{}). At 8B, the ranking is \cls{} (89.94) $>$ \traj{} (87.70) $>$ \cwm{} (87.33). The \cls{}--\cwm{} gap widens from 1.32\pp{} at 4B to 2.62\pp{} at 8B (Figure~\ref{fig:scaling_gap}); clustered bootstrap on the 3-seed superset (42, 123, 456 for \cls{}/\cwm{}) yields $p = 0.0066$ (95\% CI $[1.80, 2.71]$), which confirms the widening is not a sampling artifact.
78	
79	At 14B (3 seeds), all three formulations converge in point estimates: \cls{} 89.93$\pm$0.80\%, \cwm{} 89.50$\pm$0.67\%, \traj{} 88.86$\pm$0.77\%. The maximum pairwise gap (\cls{}--\traj{}) narrows from 3.11\pp{} at 4B to 1.07\pp{} at 14B, within the $\pm$2\pp{} equivalence margin. Seed-level TOST (df${=}$2) is inconclusive for equivalence in both pairs ($p{=}0.076$ for \cls{}--\cwm{}, $p{=}0.187$ for \cls{}--\traj{}), reflecting the low power inherent in 3-seed comparisons; Bayesian ROPE provides moderate support ($P(\text{ROPE}){=}0.887$ and $0.780$ respectively; Appendix~\ref{app:bayesian_rope}). \cwm{}'s delayed scaling onset (flat from 4B to 8B, then +2.17\pp{} at 14B) and \traj{}'s decelerating convergence (+2.13\pp{} at 8B, +1.16\pp{} at 14B) are both consistent with capacity-dependent convergence at function level.
80
